Asymptotically flat black holes with a singular Cauchy horizon and a spacelike singularity
Maxime Van de Moortel

Abstract
In our recent work [Van de Moortel, The coexistence of null and spacelike singularities inside spherically symmetric black holes] [ 64 ] , we analyzed the transition between null and spacelike singularities in spherically symmetric dynamical black holes and demonstrated that the spacelike portion is described by a Kasner metric with positive varying exponents that degenerate to ( 1 , 0 , 0 ) (1,0,0) near the null-spacelike transition. In the present paper, we provide examples of global spacetimes satisfying the local assumptions of [ 64 ] and apply its analysis to obtain a large class of asymptotically flat (spherically symmetric) black hole spacetimes that exhibit coexisting null and spacelike singularities as described in [ 64 ] . Our main results include: 1. The construction of one-ended asymptotically flat black hole spacetimes solving the Einstein–Maxwell-charged-scalar-field equations. The proof relies on a new spacelike-characteristic gluing method between any uncharged spherically symmetric solution to the event horizon of a charged dynamical black hole. 2. The construction of a large class of two-ended asymptotically flat black hole spacetimes solving the Einstein–Maxwell-(uncharged)-scalar-field equations. In both cases, we show that the terminal boundary in the black hole interior only has two distinct components: a weakly singular (null) Cauchy horizon 𝒞 ​ ℋ i + \mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}} where curvature blows up and a strong singularity 𝒮 = { r = 0 } \mathcal{S}=\{r=0\} . Our construction provides the first examples of black holes with coexisting null and spacelike singularities. These examples hold particular significance in the one-ended case as a model of gravitational collapse, where this phenomenon is conjecturally generic for the Einstein-scalar-field model, even beyond spherical symmetry.



1
Introduction

The nature of the singularity inside a realistic black hole formed from gravitational collapse remains one of the most profound open problems in General Relativity. While the influential Oppenheimer-Snyder spacetime
[
53
]
provided an early model for a dynamical black hole with a purely spacelike singularity, this scenario has been shown to be
highly non-generic in gravitational collapse
. Indeed, recent mathematical results for the Einstein vacuum equations establish that the black hole’s terminal boundary must include a null component (a Cauchy horizon,
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
)
[
27
]
, and therefore cannot be entirely spacelike. This progress in the understanding of dynamical black holes has thus led to the following conjecture (see
[
66
]
).

Conjecture 1.1

(
[
23
,
26
,
41
,
62
]
)
.

The black hole terminal boundary in generic gravitational collapse consists of a (weak) null singularity – the Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
– and a spacelike singularity
\mathcal{S}
(Figure
1
).

Yet, despite decades of progress, no black hole solution exhibiting the singularity structure of Conjecture
1.1
has ever been constructed. This paper provides the first such construction, which consists of spherically symmetric (one or two-ended) asymptotically flat black hole solutions of the Einstein–Maxwell-charged-scalar-field system:

Ric_{\mu\nu}(g)-\frac{1}{2}R(g)g_{\mu\nu}=\mathbb{T}^{EM}_{\mu\nu}+\mathbb{T}^{CSF}_{\mu\nu},

(1.1)

\mathbb{T}^{EM}_{\mu\nu}=2\left(g^{\alpha\beta}F_{\alpha\nu}F_{\beta\mu}-\frac{1}{4}F^{\alpha\beta}F_{\alpha\beta}g_{\mu\nu}\right),\ \hskip 2.84526pt\mathbb{T}^{CSF}_{\mu\nu}=2\left(\Re(D_{\mu}\phi\overline{D_{\nu}\phi})\ -\frac{1}{2}(g^{\alpha\beta}D_{\alpha}\phi\overline{D_{\beta}\phi})g_{\mu\nu}\right),

(1.2)

\nabla^{\mu}F_{\mu\nu}=\frac{q_{0}}{2}i(\phi\overline{D_{\nu}\phi}-\overline{\phi}D_{\nu}\phi),\ \hskip 2.84526ptF=dA,\ \hskip 2.84526ptD_{\mu}=\nabla_{\mu}+iq_{0}A_{\mu},

(1.3)

g^{\mu\nu}D_{\mu}D_{\nu}\phi=0.

(1.4)

These black holes have a terminal boundary consisting of a weakly singular null Cauchy horizon (
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
) and a strong singularity (
\mathcal{S}=\{r=0\}
) as depicted in Figure
1
.
Our construction offers a new and arguably more realistic global model of gravitational collapse, providing a contrast to the Oppenheimer-Snyder scenario.

Figure 1:
Penrose diagram of the gravitational collapse (=one-ended) spacetimes obtained in Theorem
II
.
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
is a weakly singular Cauchy horizon and
\mathcal{S}=\{r=0\}
is a strong singularity, spacelike near
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}\cap\mathcal{S}
and
b_{\Gamma}
.

In a broader context, the goal of the present paper is to apply the local analysis of our recent work
[
64
]
to a global, asymptotically flat setting, and obtain black hole solutions of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) possessing both a spacelike singularity and a weakly singular null Cauchy horizon whose transition is described by the analysis in
[
64
]
.

We recall the main result in
[
64
]
that precisely describes the solution of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) in this local region.

Theorem I
.

[Theorem I. in
[
64
]
]. Consider local initial data in the interior of a black hole consisting of an ingoing cone
\underline{C}_{in}
and an outgoing cone
C_{out}
terminating at the sphere of a weakly singular Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
, and denote
\mathcal{B}
the terminal boundary of the resulting solution of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
). Assume a Cauchy horizon breakdown with no locally-naked singularity, i.e.,
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}=\{v=+\infty\}\underset{\neq}{\subset}\mathcal{B}
,
and there exists
s>1
such that the following hold:

v^{-s}\lesssim|D_{v}\phi|_{|C_{out}}(v)\lesssim v^{-s},\ |\Im(\bar{\phi}D_{v}\phi)|_{|C_{out}}(v)\ll v^{-s},\ |D^{2}_{vv}\phi|_{|C_{out}}(v)\lesssim v^{-s-1}\text{ as }v\rightarrow+\infty.

(1.5)

Then,
\mathcal{B}
contains a spacelike singularity
\mathcal{S}\neq\emptyset
intersecting
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
as depicted in Figure
2
and the metric near
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}\cap\mathcal{S}
is approximated by a Kasner metric of
v
-dependent positive Kasner exponents
(1-2p(u,v),p(u,v),p(u,v))
with

p(u,v)\approx\frac{1}{v}\text{ as }v\rightarrow+\infty.

(1.6)

Figure 2:
Penrose diagram of the solution obtained in Theorem
I
with bifurcate initial data
\underline{C}_{in}\cup C_{out}
.

Examples of gravitational collapse black holes

We now present our main result for gravitational collapse: the construction of one-ended, asymptotically flat black holes realizing the spacelike/null singularity structure of Conjecture
1.1
while additionally analyzing the spacelike/null transition as an application of Theorem
I
. While the scope of our rigorous analysis is restricted to the spherically symmetric model of equations (
1.1
)–(
1.4
), we provide heuristic evidence in Section
1.1
that a generic rotating black hole will possess similar features. Theorem
II
below is our main result, and a simplified version of Theorem
3.5
which can be found in Section
3
.

Theorem II
.

There exists a large class of spherically symmetric one-ended asymptotically flat black hole solutions of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with
q_{0}\neq 0
, with a regular center
\Gamma\neq\emptyset
satisfying the following properties:

•

The Penrose diagram is given by Figure
1
, namely the terminal boundary only has two non-empty components: a weakly singular null Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}\neq~\emptyset
, and a crushing singularity
\mathcal{S}=\{r=0\}
.

•

\mathcal{S}
is spacelike in a neighborhood of
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}\cap\mathcal{S}
, and obeys the degenerating Kasner asymptotics of Theorem
I
.

•

\mathcal{S}
is spacelike in a neighborhood of
\Gamma
and spatially-homogeneous, described by an asymptotically Kasner metric of exponents
(\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{3})
.

Remark 1.1
.

We already note that, to study spherically symmetric one-ended solutions of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with
F\neq 0
(which is essential to have
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}\neq\emptyset

[
11
]
), one must consider a charged scalar field, i.e.,
q_{0}\neq 0
, see e.g.
[
41
,
66
]
.

New spherically symmetric gluing results

Proving Theorem
II
requires surmounting the challenge of constructing a
global
asymptotically flat black hole solution, while maintaining sufficiently precise control over the dynamics in order to satisfy the assumptions of Theorem
I
in the black hole interior.

To achieve this, we introduce a novel gluing strategy allowing the construction of one-ended black holes with
any spacelike singularity near the center
combined with
any prescribed
event-horizon late-time tail decaying at an inverse-polynomial rate. Theorem
1.2
below, a rough version of Theorem
6.10
, describes this.

Theorem 1.2

(Black hole construction by gluing)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\mathcal{M}_{L}
a one-ended spherically symmetric black hole solution of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with
F\equiv 0
and
\phi_{H}(v)
satisfying the decay assumptions, for some
s>\frac{3}{2}

|\phi_{H}|(v),\ |D_{v}\phi_{H}|(v)\lesssim[1+|v|]^{-s}\text{ as }v\rightarrow+\infty.

(1.7)

Then, there exists
\mathcal{M}
a one-ended asymptotically flat spherically symmetric black hole
C^{k}
solution of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with
F\neq 0
,
q_{0}\neq 0
satisfying the following properties:

•

Near the center
\Gamma
,
\mathcal{M}
coincides with
\mathcal{M}_{L}
.

•

There exists
0<|e|<M
such that the black hole relaxes to a Reissner–Nordström solution of mass
M
and charge
e
. Moreover, the metric and scalar field are
C^{k}
regular across and on the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

•

In Eddington–Finkelstein coordinate
v
, the scalar field coincides with
\phi_{H}(v)
on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, i.e.,

\phi_{|\mathcal{H}^{+}}(v)=\phi_{H}(v).

(1.8)

We then obtain Theorem
II
as an application of Theorem
1.2
where
\mathcal{M}_{L}
is a FLRW metric featuring a spacelike singularity, which, by design, is spatially-homogeneous (see Section
1.4
).

Remark 1.2
.

One can also apply Theorem
1.2
where
\mathcal{M}_{L}
is an uncharged naked singularity, as constructed by Christodoulou in
[
13
]
. The result is a black hole spacetime with a locally naked singularity (see Section
1.3
).

The attentive reader may wonder whether Theorem
1.2
explicitly relates to gluing problems. It turns out that Theorem
1.2
emerges as the outcome of a more general gluing strategy within spherical symmetry. We extract the main new gluing statement we obtain (see Section
2.6
and Theorem
3.9
for a more precise version):

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Any regular uncharged data sphere can be spatially glued to an exact Reissner–Nordström trapped surface as a
C^{k}
solution to the spacelike constraint equations induced by (
1.1
)–(
1.4
).

The gluing Theorem
1.3
, while formulated as a spacelike gluing result, is part of a broader gluing strategy that involves both spacelike and characteristic gluing, see Section
1.4
. The main novelty of our approach is to exploit spherical symmetry and combine methods from spacelike and characteristic gluing to operate deep inside the trapped region of the black hole—a regime inaccessible to previous perturbative gluing methods.

Theorem
1.3
will also be applied to construct analogues of the Oppenheimer–Snyder spacetime for the Einstein-scalar-field system (
1.1
)–(
1.4
), both in the uncharged case with a Schwarzschild exterior (Figure
5
) and in the charged case with a Reissner–Nordström exterior (Figure
6
), see Theorem
1.5
. It should already be noted that while these Oppenheimer–Snyder analogues are of great historical interest, they are expected to be
non-generic
under Conjecture
1.1
(see the discussion following Theorem
1.5
).

Finally, we remark that Theorem
1.2
is not yet sufficient to apply Theorem
I
. Our gluing method indeed allows us to
construct a global spacetime
with the desired large-scale properties, but we must still
verify
that this construction satisfies the precise local dynamics required by Theorem
I
, specifically the assumptions (
1.5
) on an outgoing cone strictly to the future of the event horizon. To propagate estimates between the event horizon and the cone on which (
1.5
) should hold, we use (a slightly refined version of) the scattering theory developed by the author and Kehle
[
40
]
for (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with
q_{0}\neq 0
. This will also be discussed in Section
1.5
.

Examples of two-ended black holes

We now turn to global applications of Theorem
I
for (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with
q_{0}=0
(uncharged scalar field). In this case, a spherically symmetric solution of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with
F\not\equiv 0
is necessarily two-ended (see e.g.
[
41
]
). In the two-ended case, however, there exist stable spherically symmetric solutions of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with no spacelike singularity as showed by Dafermos
[
25
]
(left-most Penrose diagram in Figure
3
). On the other hand, our result in the two-ended case only applies to solutions in which there exists a non-empty singularity
\mathcal{S}=\{r=0\}
(right-most Penrose diagram in Figure
3
).

Figure 3:
Left: Two-ended black hole with a Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
and no spacelike singularity.

Right: Two-ended black hole with coexisting Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
and singularity
\mathcal{S}=\{r=0\}
(Theorem
III
).

Contrary to the charged case
q_{0}\neq 0
, the late-time tail theory of generic spherically symmetric solutions of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) for
q_{0}=0
is well-developed, thanks to results by Dafermos–Rodnianski
[
28
]
, Luk–Oh
[
46
,
47
]
and most recently Gautam
[
33
]
. We leverage these results to apply the analysis of Theorem
I
to the subclass of generic spherically symmetric two-ended solutions of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) whose Penrose diagram is as in the right-most of Figure
3
, and we construct the
first examples such that
\mathcal{S}\neq\emptyset
for this model
1
1

1

The author, however, previously constructed two-ended black holes with a Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
and a crushing singularity
\mathcal{S}=\{r=0\}
for (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with a massive scalar field
[
63
]
and with Li for a charged scalar field
[
43
]
, i.e., (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with
q_{0}\neq 0
.
.

Theorem III
.

There exists a large class of spherically symmetric two-ended asymptotically flat black hole solution of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) for
q_{0}=0
satisfying the following properties:

•

The Penrose diagram is given by the right panel of Figure
3
, namely the terminal boundary only has two non-empty components: a weakly singular null Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}\neq~\emptyset
, and a crushing singularity
\mathcal{S}=\{r=0\}
.

•

\mathcal{S}
is spacelike in a neighborhood of
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}\cap\mathcal{S}
, and obeys the Kasner asymptotics of Theorem
I
.

Not only does Theorem
III
construct the first black holes with the right Penrose diagram of Figure
3
, i.e.,
\mathcal{S}\neq\emptyset
, but it also provides the first non-trivial quantitative estimates on the junction between the spacelike singularity
\mathcal{S}=\{r=0\}
and the Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
in a two-ended asymptotically flat black hole.

Conditional global applications of Theorem
I

A secondary objective of the present manuscript is to find the most general global conditions on a spherically symmetric black hole so the conclusions of Theorem
I
(which is a local result) hold. In essence, we prove that this is the case as long as there exist one singular sphere with zero area-radius on the terminal boundary (or, equivalently,
\mathcal{S}\neq\emptyset
in the language of Theorem
3.2
).

Theorem IV
.

Consider a black hole spacetime with a Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}\neq\emptyset
and assume that (
1.5
) holds on any outgoing cone
C_{out}
under the Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
. Then, under the following respective conditions:

1.

(One-ended case). Assume the absence of a locally-naked singularity emanating from the center
\Gamma
.

2.

(Two-ended case). Assume that the spacetime is described by the rightmost Penrose diagram of Figure
3
, i.e.,
\mathcal{S}\neq\emptyset
.

Then, the terminal boundary only has two components:
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
, which is weakly singular in the sense that the Hawking mass is infinite on
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
, and a crushing singularity
\mathcal{S}=\{r=0\}
, which is spacelike in a neighborhood of
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}\cap\mathcal{S}
and obeys the degenerating Kasner asymptotics of Theorem
I
.

Theorem
IV
corresponds
2
2

2

We note, however, that Theorem
3.4
and Theorem
3.7
are slightly stronger results than Theorem
IV
as stated in that they only require (
1.5
) to hold on
one
outgoing cone
C_{out}
under
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
so that the conclusion of Theorem
I
applies.
to Theorem
3.4
(one-ended case) and Theorem
3.7
(two-ended case).

Remark 1.3
.

As noted in Remark
1.2
, there exist one-ended black holes with both a Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
and a locally naked singularity
\mathcal{CH}_{\Gamma}
. However, we note that locally naked singularities are conjecturally non-generic; this is related to Weak Cosmic Censorship for the model (
1.1
)–(
1.4
), see
[
41
,
66
]
or
[
64
]
, Section 1.3 for details.

As part of the proof of Theorem
IV
, we obtain an intermediate result of independent interest, excluding the presence of certain terminal boundary components that were a priori possible, see Figure
7
and Section
1.3
.

•

in the one-ended case, the Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
cannot be the only terminal boundary component, as in the Penrose diagram of Figure
8
(this result was already obtained in
[
62
]
where we proved the breakdown of the Cauchy horizon; however, the new proof presented here is shorter and more constructive).

•

There is no ingoing collapsed cone
\mathcal{S}_{i^{+}}
on which
r\equiv 0
that extends the Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
to the future, as depicted in Figure
7
(more precisely, we prove
\mathcal{S}_{i^{+}}=\emptyset
under the assumptions of Theorem
I
).

Remark 1.4
.

It is also of interest to consider the Einstein–Maxwell equations coupled to a massive and charged scalar field. The main result of
[
64
]
, Theorem
I
, is also valid in this case, as are the conditional results presented in Theorem
IV
. However, the construction of one or two-ended asympotically flat black holes provided in Theorem
II
or Theorem
III
do not work in the massive case, where late-time tails are more subtle, see
[
54
,
58
]
.

1.1
Models of gravitational collapse: Oppenheimer–Snyder’s solution and beyond

1.1.1
The Oppenheimer–Snyder spacetime

The rigorous theoretical modeling of gravitational collapse that predicted the dynamical formation of a black hole began with the celebrated Oppenheimer-Snyder
[
53
]
spacetime in 1939. Their construction is a spherically symmetric solution of the Einstein equations coupled with a homogeneous ball of dust (the density of dust is constant inside a ball of fixed radius
R_{b}
and zero outside). Its Penrose diagram is as in Figure
4
: a black hole whose terminal boundary only contains the spacelike singularity
\mathcal{S}
. We make some observations on the Oppenheimer–Snyder spacetime:

•

Outside of the ball of fixed radius
R_{b}
, the Oppenheimer–Snyder spacetime is exactly Schwarzschild, thus
\mathcal{S}
coincides with Schwarzschild’s spacelike singularity, with bounded Jacobi fields in the orthoradial direction but an unbounded Jacobi field in the radial one (also known as “spaghettification”, see
[
34
,
51
,
52
]
).

•

Inside of the ball of fixed radius
R_{b}
,
\mathcal{S}=\{r=0\}
is isotropic, coinciding with a FLRW singularity.

•

The Oppenheimer–Snyder spacetime is the MGHD of one-ended asymptotically flat
W^{1,\infty}\cap C^{0}
initial data. As such, it only solves the Einstein-dust equations in a weak (distributional) sense.

Figure 4:
Penrose diagram of the Oppenheimer–Snyder solution of the Einstein-dust equations.

Despite its historical importance, the Oppenheimer–Snyder spacetime is not generic, even among spherically symmetric solutions of the Einstein-dust model: Christodoulou
[
10
]
indeed showed that even a slightly inhomogeneous ball of dust collapses into a naked singularity instead of a black hole, due to dust shells crossing.

1.1.2
Einstein-scalar-field gravitational collapse in spherical symmetry

In a series of papers
[
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
]
, Christodoulou famously studied spherically symmetric solutions of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with
F\equiv 0
, a model known as Einstein-scalar-field, and which is free from shell-crossing singularities. He considered the gravitational collapse case (one-ended asymptotically flat) and showed the following results:

•

Solutions of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) (for rough initial data) with a naked (or locally naked) singularity exist
[
13
]
.

•

Such (locally)-naked singularity solutions, however, are unstable
[
14
]
within a class of rough solutions.

•

For generic rough initial data, the terminal boundary of the black hole is a spacelike singularity
\mathcal{S}

[
11
]
.

Moreover, it was later shown that (smooth) black holes for this model converge to Schwarzschild
[
2
,
28
,
47
]
in some sense towards timelike infinity
i^{+}
; in particular,
\mathcal{S}
also exhibits the above-mentioned “spaghettification” near
i^{+}
. Away from
i^{+}
, it was shown generally that
\mathcal{S}
locally adopts a Kasner form
[
42
]
with varying exponents.

We also mention the uncharged Kehle–Unger spherically symmetric gluing methods
[
39
]
, allowing to construct a one-ended black hole whose event horizon is exactly Schwarszschild at late times (see also Section
1.4
).

Nonetheless, to the best of the author’s knowledge, there is no known analogue to the Oppenheimer–Snyder solution for (
1.1
)–(
1.4
), in the sense of a one-ended asymptotically flat black hole whose terminal boundary is a spacelike singularity coinciding with Schwarzschild’s in a open neighborhood of
i^{+}
, as depicted in Figure
5
. Theorem
1.2
, however, allows for such a construction (see Theorem
1.5
), as we will explain in Section
1.1.3
.

Remark 1.5
.

Even though Christodoulou proved that generic (among rough data solutions) black holes only have a spacelike singularity
[
14
]
, it is not known how to construct any
C^{2}
black hole solution of (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) with this feature. This is because the strategy used in
[
14
]
to generate (generic) examples of such black holes is based on a perturbation argument of a (locally)naked singularity. The gluing strategy employed in Theorem
1.2
, on the other hand, offers a method to construct more concrete examples with arbitrarily high
C^{k}
-regularity.

Figure 5:
Penrose diagram of the Oppenheimer–Snyder like uncharged solution of (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) in Theorem
1.5
for
q=0
.
\mathcal{S}
is exactly Schwarzschild’s singularity near
i^{+}
, and exactly the FLRW singularity near
b_{\Gamma}
. Note the absence of a Cauchy horizon (
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}=\emptyset
), in contrast to the examples of Theorem
II
(depicted in Figure
1
).

1.1.3
Einstein–Maxwell-charged-scalar-field gravitational collapse in spherical symmetry

The study of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) in the charged case
F\neq 0
is in parts motivated by its resemblance with the Einstein equations in vacuum, see the discussions in
[
64
,
66
]
. In particular, as we will elaborate in Section
1.1.4
, dynamical rotating black holes admit a Cauchy horizon from infinity
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
, in the same way dynamical charged black holes do
[
24
,
59
]
. The author extensively studied the black hole interior for the (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) in spherical symmetry
[
40
,
59
,
60
,
62
,
64
]
and has shown the following results for dynamical black holes converging to Reissner–Nordström (see Theorem
1.9
below and Section
1.3
for more precise versions of the statements):

•

There exists a Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
, which is
C^{0}
-regular under physical assumptions on the event horizon.

•

\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
is
C^{2}
-singular under physical assumptions on the event horizon (
weak singularity
).

•

Due to the weak singularity,
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
breaks down in finite retarded-time. Therefore, there is another terminal boundary component, which could be a crushing singularity
\mathcal{S}
, or a locally naked singularity
\mathcal{CH}_{\Gamma}
.

•

If we assume
\mathcal{CH}_{\Gamma}=\emptyset
, then
\mathcal{S}
is spacelike and tidally contracting near
\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
, in the sense that all its Jacobi fields are bounded (or equivalently, its generalized Kasner exponents are positive).

•

As a conclusion, if
\mathcal{CH}_{\Gamma}=\emptyset
, the only terminal boundary components of the black hole are
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
.

The attentive reader will have recognized a rephrasing of the first statement of Theorem
IV
in the outcome of the enumeration. We point out that Theorem
II
precisely provides (one-ended asymptotically flat) examples such that
\mathcal{CH}_{\Gamma}=\emptyset
, and described as above; in addition, the crushing singularity
\mathcal{S}
is spatially homogeneous near the center
\Gamma
, similarly to the Oppenheimer–Snyder case. However, these examples provide a
model of gravitational collapse competing
with Oppenheimer–Snyder’s with two essential differences that we emphasize:

•

The Cauchy horizon from infinity
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
, leading to the Kasner exponents of
\mathcal{S}
degenerating at
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}\cap\mathcal{S}
.

•

The absence of unbounded Jacobi fields (a.k.a spaghettification, or negative Kasner exponents) near
i^{+}
.

As we discuss in Section
1.3
, it is expected that
\mathcal{CH}_{\Gamma}=\emptyset
for generic solutions, leading to the following conjecture
3
3

3

The conjectured tidally contracting character of the spacelike singularity goes back to the celebrated BKL heuristics on the stability of spacelike singularities
[
6
,
7
]
, see the discussion in
[
64
]
, Section 1.6.
, which is more specific than our introductory Conjecture
1.1
.

Conjecture 1.4
.

Generic (regular) spherically symmetric one-ended asymptotically flat black hole solutions for (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with
q_{0}\neq 0
have only two terminal boundary components:

•

the Cauchy horizon from infinity
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
, a weak null singularity.

•

the crushing singularity
\mathcal{S}=\{r=0\}
, which is spacelike and tidally contracting.

In other words, it is conjectured that the examples of Theorem
II
reflect the properties of generic black holes. Proving the validity of Conjecture
1.4
requires, among other things, a resolution of Weak Cosmic Censorship for charged scalar field (the analogue of Christodoulou’s work in the uncharged case
[
11
,
13
,
14
]
), open at present.

We now return to the construction of Oppenheimer–Snyder like solutions already mentioned in Section
1.1.2
. Our goal is to produce a spatially-homogeneous spacelike singularity near the center
\Gamma
, and exactly isometric to Schwarzschild (or Reissner–Nordström in the charged case) in an open neighborhood of
i^{+}
that includes part of the trapped region.
The following theorem, a simplified version of Corollary
6.8
(see also Corollary
6.2
for the uncharged version), carries out such a construction for
C^{k}
solutions, where
k
is arbitrarily large.

Theorem 1.5
.

(Oppenheimer–Snyder analogues with a scalar field).
For any regularity index
k\in\mathbb{N}
and charge to mass ratio
q\in[0,1)
, there exist
C^{k}
spherically symmetric one-ended asymptotically flat black hole solutions of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with
q_{0}\neq 0
, with a regular center
\Gamma\neq\emptyset
and satisfying the following:

•

There exists an open neighborhood of
i^{+}
(timelike infinity),
\mathcal{I}^{+}
(null infinity) and
i^{0}
(spacelike infinity) in which the spacetime coincides with a Reissner–Nordström metric of charge ratio
\pm qM_{f}
, for some
M_{f}>0
(Schwarzschild if
q=0
). Thus, at late enough times, the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
is exactly Reissner–Nordström.

•

If
q=0
, the Penrose diagram is given by Figure
5
, namely the terminal boundary only has one non-empty component: a spacelike singularity
\mathcal{S}=\{r=0\}
.

•

If
q\in(0,1)
, the Penrose diagram is given by Figure
6
, namely the terminal boundary only has two non-empty components: a null Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}\neq~\emptyset
, and a crushing singularity
\mathcal{S}=\{r=0\}
.

•

\mathcal{S}
is spacelike in a neighborhood of
\Gamma
and spatially-homogeneous, described by an asymptotically Kasner metric of exponents
(\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{3})
.

The examples presented in Theorem
1.5
are known to be non-generic
[
66
]
and violate Conjecture
1.1
, since
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}=\emptyset
in the case
q=0
, and
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}\neq\emptyset
is
non-singular
near
i^{+}
(since it coincides with Reissner–Nordström) in the case
q\neq 0
, in contrast to the spacetimes of Theorem
II
. This distinction prevents the application of Theorem
I
and leaves the quantitative coexistence of the spacelike and null singularities unresolved. Despite the fact that the examples of Theorem
1.5
violate Conjecture
1.1
, our proof of Theorem
II

builds up
on that of Theorem
1.5
, which already illustrates the heart of our gluing strategy (see Section
1.4
).

Remark 1.6
.

In Theorem
1.5
, we allow for an arbitrary choice of
q\in[0,1)
and the resulting spacetime is initially free of trapped surfaces, consistently with our modelization of gravitational collapse (see Section
1.3
). However, if we want to construct a spacetime such that the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
lies strictly to the future of the initial data as depicted in Figure
5
and Figure
6
, we must restrict
q
to a small value, see Corollary
6.8
.

Figure 6:
Penrose diagram of the Oppenheimer–Snyder like charged solution of (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) obtained in Theorem
1.5
for
q\neq 0
.
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
is a Reissner–Nordström Cauchy horizon near
i^{+}
, and exactly FLRW near
b_{\Gamma}
. Note that
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
is not (everywhere) singular, in contrast to the examples of Theorem
II
.

1.1.4
Einstein-scalar-field gravitational collapse outside of spherical symmetry

The dynamics in the black hole interior is less understood outside of spherical symmetry. A major result by Dafermos–Luk
[
27
]
established that small perturbations of Kerr spacetimes develop a non-empty Cauchy horizon
(\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}})
, and the work of Luk
[
44
]
provided the first examples of such Cauchy horizons containing weak null singularities. While these findings resolve the nature of the terminal boundary near
i^{+}
, the global interior structure—particularly the presence of a spacelike singularity—remains an open problem central to an eventual resolution of the Strong Cosmic Censorship Conjecture (see Section
1.2
). This question is conjectured to be particularly delicate
in vacuum
, as no dynamically-stable spacelike singularities are known for the Einstein equations, except if we restrict the dynamics to non-generic symmetry classes
[
1
,
30
]
. This is consistent with the BKL heuristics, which predict chaotic dynamics rather than a stable singular terminal boundary
[
6
,
7
]
.

The result of Dafermos–Luk
[
27
]
notably also extends
4
4

4

While this is not explicitly stated in
[
27
]
, it can be inferred from the estimates of
[
27
]
, together the methods to handle the scalar field on the Kerr black hole interior, see
[
31
]
.
to the Einstein-scalar-field model (i.e., (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) with
F\equiv 0
). Contrary to the vacuum case, however, spacelike singularities are better understood in the presence of a scalar field. In this setting, the BKL heuristics
[
6
,
7
]
identify certain spatially-homogeneous solutions (a sub-class of Kasner spacetimes
[
37
]
, solutions of Einstein-scalar-field) as stable, and Fournodavlos, Rodnianski, and Speck indeed proved this
[
30
]
. The tidally contracting spacelike singularity (positive Kasner exponents) present in these Kasner models thus provides a plausible model for the structure of generic spacelike singularities.

In the spherically symmetric case, the spacelike singularity
\mathcal{S}
addressed by Theorem
I
is indeed tidally contractive (positive Kasner exponents), consistently with the general scalar field expectation. However, its construction cannot not be addressed by the methods of
[
30
]
due to a crucial technical distinction. The stability analysis in
[
30
]
, as well as other known constructions
[
29
]
, requires non-degenerating Kasner exponents. In contrast, the singularity
\mathcal{S}
in Theorem
I
exhibits Kasner exponents that degenerate towards the null/spacelike transition sphere
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}\cap\mathcal{S}
, a phenomenon that requires specific new estimates in the proof of Theorem
I
.

We conjecture that this degeneration is not an artifact of spherical symmetry but instead a key feature of the general problem. This is analogous to the study of the Cauchy horizon, where the full dynamical treatment for rotating black holes in vacuum
[
27
]
built upon foundational insights from the spherically symmetric case
[
22
,
24
,
45
,
59
]
. Motivated by this, we propose a generalization of Conjecture
1.4
to the Einstein-scalar-field system (i.e, (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with
F\equiv 0
) outside of spherical symmetry. This constitutes a strengthened version of Conjecture
1.1
, and we hope to return to this and related problems in future work.

1.2
Applications to the Strong Cosmic Censorship Conjecture

The celebrated Strong Cosmic Censorship Conjecture of Penrose
[
56
]
is a central motivation to study the interior of dynamical black holes, see the related discussion in our previous work
[
64
]
or in the review
[
66
]
. Here, we focus on a simplified version of Strong Cosmic Censorship within spherically symmetric solutions of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) (see
[
15
,
25
,
41
,
66
]
), formulated in the context of gravitational collapse following Penrose
[
55
,
56
,
57
]
.

Conjecture 1.6

(
C^{2}
Strong Cosmic Censorship)
.

The Maximal Globally Hyperbolic Development for (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) of generic, one-ended asymptotically
flat complete spherically symmetric initial data is

C^{2}
-(future)-inextendible.

Conjecture
1.6
remains open at present. The charged spacetimes of Theorem
1.5
are smoothly extendible across an open subset of
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
and thus violate the statement of Conjecture
1.6
, but as we explained, they are non-generic. It is instructive to notice, however, that the spacetimes constructed in Theorem
II
and Theorem
III
(more generally, those of Theorem
IV
) are
C^{2}
-inextendible, and thus respect the paradigm of Conjecture
1.6
.

Theorem 1.7
.

The one-ended or two-ended asymptotically flat spacetimes obeying the assumptions of Theorem
IV
are
C^{2}
-(future)-inextendible. In particular, the one-ended examples of Theorem
II
and the two-ended examples of Theorem
III
are
C^{2}
-(future)-inextendible.

Therefore, the final step in proving Conjecture
1.6
is to show that generic one-ended spherically symmetric solutions of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) uphold the assumptions of Theorem
IV
, which is indeed conjectured (recall Section
1.1
).

1.3
Qualitative results on the terminal boundary and Cauchy horizon breakdown

Mathematical setting of gravitational collapse

Gravitational collapse (see
[
66
]
, Section 5 or
[
64
]
, Section 1.3-1.4) is modeled by the MGHD of asymptotically flat initial data
(\Sigma,g)
with one-end, assuming that
\Sigma
is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and thus has a center
\Gamma
corresponding to the origin of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We also assume that
\Sigma
is free from anti-trapped surfaces or trapped surfaces.
Note that the spacetimes constructed in Theorem
II
are of gravitational collapse type, while those of Theorem
III
, which are two-ended asymptotically flat, are not.

Figure 7:
General Penrose diagram of a one-ended spherically symmetric black hole solution after Theorem
3.2
.

A priori characterization of the black hole terminal boundary

For spherically symmetric black hole solutions of (
1.1
)-(
1.4
), it is possible to characterize the future boundary of the MGHD (also called terminal boundary)
[
24
,
41
]
:
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is a component of the terminal boundary if either of the following conditions is satisfied:

A.

(
Crushing singularity
). The area-radius
r
extends to
0
on
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
(corresponds to
\mathcal{S}
,
\mathcal{S}^{1}_{\Gamma}
,
\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\Gamma}
or
\mathcal{S}_{i^{+}}
in Figure
7
).

B.

(
Cauchy horizon from infinity
).
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is a null segment emanating from timelike infinity
i^{+}
(corresponds to
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
or
\mathcal{S}_{i^{+}}
in Figure
7
).

C.

(
Locally naked singularity
) [Only if
\Gamma\neq\emptyset
].
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is a null segment emanating from the center
\Gamma
(corresponds to
\mathcal{CH}_{\Gamma}
,
\mathcal{S}^{1}_{\Gamma}
or
\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\Gamma}
in Figure
7
).

This is formalized by Theorem
3.2
(originally from
[
41
]
) in the one-ended case, and Theorem
3.6
(originally from
[
24
]
) in the two-ended case. Note that, in the two-ended case, there is no center (i.e.,
\Gamma=\emptyset
) and therefore Case
C
is impossible. In the one-ended case, however, Case
C
is possible (Remark
1.2
) although we recall Christoudoulou proved that Case
C
is non-generic
[
14
]
for (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) with
F\equiv 0
in spherical symmetry.

Breakdown of the Cauchy horizon: old and new

A gravitational collapse scenario in which a Cauchy horizon from infinity
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
is the only terminal boundary component implies in particular that
\mathcal{S}=\emptyset
in Figure
7
, corresponding to the absence of any spacelike singularity. Such examples where the Cauchy horizon closes-off the spacetime, as depicted in Figure
8
, can be rigorously constructed if the Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
is non-singular
[
39
]
. However, the author proved in
[
59
,
60
]
that
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
is (weakly) singular under generic assumptions on the event horizon and subsequently in
[
62
]
that, due to this singularity, the Cauchy horizon cannot close-off the spacetime, i.e.,
\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{S}^{1}_{\Gamma}\cup\mathcal{CH}_{\Gamma}\neq\emptyset
in Figure
7
. This result–
the breakdown of weak null singularities
–is the starting point of the investigation of the transition between null and spacelike singularities initiated in our work
[
64
]
.

Figure 8:
The impossible Penrose diagram if
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
is weakly singular, as a consequence of the result of
[
62
]
.

In
[
62
]
, the breakdown of the Cauchy horizon was proved using an argument by contradiction which did not provide any quantitative estimates on the spacelike singularity
\mathcal{S}
. It can be summarized as follows:

•

The Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
is surrounded by the trapped region, due to the blow-up of the Hawking mass.

•

Assuming that
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
closes off the spacetime, i.e., that the Penrose diagram is given by Figure
8
, there exists a sequence of apparent horizon spheres
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converging to
b_{\Gamma}
, the endpoint of the center
\Gamma
. This is proven by interpolation between the center
\Gamma
, within the regular region, and
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
, which is trapped.

•

A charge (denoted
Q
) to mass (denoted
\varpi
) ratio inequality of the form
|Q|\ll\varpi
holds at the apparent horizon
\mathcal{A}
. The proof of this inequality uses the ingoing Raychaudhuri equation (see
[
62
]
, Section 4.1.2).

•

The charge to mass ratio inequality shows that the ingoing future of the apparent horizon
\mathcal{A}
is immediately inside the trapped region, which is a contradiction if
\mathcal{A}
is chosen to be “the left-most apparent horizon”.

In
our previous work
[
64
]
, we have developed quantitative estimates under the Cauchy horizon to handle the transition with a spacelike singularity, in order to prove Theorem
I
. In the present manuscript, we will use these estimates in the proof of Theorem
IV
and also offers a shorter, more constructive proof of the breakdown of the Cauchy horizon originally proven in
[
62
]
. Essentially, we will prove the following (see Proposition
4.1
):

Theorem 1.8
.

Let
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
be a Cauchy horizon from infinity such that, in Eddington–Finkelstein coordinates, the Hawking mass blows up at the following exponential rate, where
K_{-}<0
and
s>\frac{1}{2}
: there exists
u
such that

\varpi(u,v)\gtrsim_{u}e^{2|K_{-}|v}v^{-2s}.

(1.9)

Then, there exists an outgoing cone
C_{0}
intersecting
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
, such that the entire causal rectangle generated by
C_{0}
and
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
is within the trapped region. In particular, this causal rectangle does not intersect the center
\Gamma
.

Figure 9:
The trapped region
\mathcal{T}
under the Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
, extending to its future end-sphere.

The major difference between Theorem
1.8
and the older strategy of
[
62
]
is that the size of the trapped region does not shrink towards the endpoint of the Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
, which is remarkable and immediately disallows a spacetime geometry where
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
closes off the spacetime as depicted in Figure
8
. The required assumption (
1.9
) is, however, more quantitative and demanding than the qualitative Hawking mass blow-up from
[
62
]
. Theorem
1.8
will be applied to the one-ended global setting as part of Theorem
3.4
.

Unexpectedly, Theorem
1.8
will also play a crucial in constructing examples of
two-ended spacetimes
with a spacelike singularity
\mathcal{S}\neq\emptyset
, an essential step in the proof of Theorem
III
, see Section
7
.

1.4
A novel gluing theorem for spacetimes relaxing to Reissner–Nordström

Gluing methods are routinely used in General Relativity to construct initial data on a spacelike hypersurface that satisfy the constraints equations imposed by (
1.1
), see e.g.,
[
8
,
16
]
or the reviews
[
9
,
19
]
for further details and references. In the black hole setting, we mention the notable results
[
17
,
18
,
20
]
allowing to glue a general solution to the Kerr black hole outside of a compact region and recent progress on gluing two black hole initial data sets
[
35
]
. See also
[
36
,
49
,
50
]
for a novel class of spacelike gluing results relying on the flexibility of the linearized theory.
In other recent developments, Aretakis, Czimek and Rodnianski designed a novel so-called
characteric gluing approach

[
3
,
4
,
5
]
allowing to construct solutions to the null constraint equations imposed by (
1.1
) on a null cone in the near-Minkowski perturbative regime, see also the more recent work of Czimek and Rodnianski on obstruction-free characteristic gluing
[
21
]
. Even more recently, the characteristic gluing approach was used by Kehle and Unger
[
38
,
39
]
in the context of black holes, who obtained event horizon gluing results.

These previous gluing results, however, are either limited to the perturbative near-Minkowski regime or, in the black hole setting, to the black hole exterior region near the asymptotically flat end, with the exception of
[
38
,
39
]
that address the event horizon. In the present manuscript, we require in contrast a gluing result deep inside the trapped region of the black hole, in the interior region. The main novelty of our approach is to rely on spherical symmetry and combine methods from spacelike and characteristic gluing to glue
any uncharged regular sphere to a dynamical black hole event horizon converging to Reissner–Nordström at any prescribed rate
.

The main application of our gluing theorems is to construct a one-ended asymptotically black hole such that:

•

The terminal boundary near
\Gamma
is
\mathcal{S}
spacelike. In particular, there is no locally naked singularity:
\mathcal{CH}_{\Gamma}=\emptyset
. In our case, we choose
\mathcal{S}
modeled after the FLRW spacelike singularity, which is spatially-homogeneous.

•

The event horizon is transversally and tangentially regular, and converges to a Reissner–Nordström event horizon at a
freely prescribed rate
, governed by a freely prescribed scalar field.

•

The assumptions of Theorem
I
, most importantly (
1.5
), are satisfied under the Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
.

To fix the terminology, let us recall the FLRW metric, a spatially-homogeneous solution of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with
F\equiv 0
and initial data diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
(note, however, that it is obviously not asymptotically flat):

g=-dt^{2}+a^{2}(t)\left(d\rho^{2}+\rho^{2}d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\right).

(1.10)

Spacetimes of the form (
1.10
) are analyzed in Proposition
6.3
, where it is shown that a spacelike singularity is formed in finite time
t=T_{S}
.
Then, we recall the Reissner–Nordström metric, a stationary solution of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) in spherical symmetry

g_{RN}=-(1-\frac{2M}{r}+\frac{e^{2}}{r^{2}})dt^{2}+(1-\frac{2M}{r}+\frac{e^{2}}{r^{2}})^{-1}dr^{2}+r^{2}(d\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}(\theta)d\varphi^{2}),

(1.11)

corresponding to
\phi\equiv 0
, and
F=\frac{e}{r^{2}}dt\wedge dr
. In the sub-extremal case
0<|e|<M
, (
1.11
) is a two-ended black hole solution whose MGHD terminates a smooth Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}=\{r=M-\sqrt{M^{2}-e^{2}}\}
.

We will now describe our new gluing strategy and sketch its main steps below, comparing it to the existing literature. The reader can also consult a more detailed outline of the construction in Section
6.1
.

1.4.1
Spatial gluing of a regular uncharged sphere to an apparent horizon

The first step is to start with a regular, uncharged sphere
\mathbb{S}_{R}
, which we will eventually choose to be FLRW from (
1.10
). To get into the trapped region, we first want to glue it to an uncharged apparent horizon using spacelike gluing techniques. Note that the spacelike constraints induced by (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) in spherical symmetry form a system of ODEs, detailed in Section
2.6
. We show the following results (see already Proposition
6.5
):

•

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, one can glue
\mathbb{S}_{R}
spatially to any apparent horizon sphere
\mathbb{S}_{A}
as a
C^{k}
solution providing

1<[R_{A}|\partial_{v}\phi|_{|\mathbb{S}_{A}}]^{2}<1+\frac{1}{k-1},

(1.12)

where
R_{A}
is the area-radius of
\mathbb{S}_{A}
, and
v
is chosen so that
\mathbb{S}_{A}
is lapse-normalized (see Section
2.6
). Moreover, this can be done through a spacelike hypersurface
\Sigma_{A}
, where
\Sigma_{A}-\mathbb{S}_{A}
is in the regular region.

•

It is
not
possible to glue
\mathbb{S}_{R}
to a Schwarzschild apparent horizon
\mathbb{S}_{A}
, since (
1.12
) would be violated.

Therefore, the above gluing procedure is flexible, since the apparent horizon sphere
\mathbb{S}_{A}
is essentially free, except for the condition (
1.12
). In particular, the scalar field ingoing derivatives can be prescribed arbitrarily at
\mathbb{S}_{A}
.

Condition (
1.12
) is reminiscent of
obstruction-free
gluing à la Czimek–Rodnianski
[
21
]
where certain
inequalities
must be satisfy to allow for the characteristic gluing of two spheres, see also
[
36
,
49
,
50
]
for a related spacelike obstruction-free gluing method. The crucial distinction in our work is that inequality (
1.12
) is a direct consequence of gluing to an apparent horizon—a feature absent from the previous works
[
21
,
36
,
49
,
50
]
.

1.4.2
Characteristic gluing of an apparent horizon to a Schwarzschild trapped surface

As a next step, we glue
\mathbb{S}_{A}
to a Schwarzschild trapped sphere
\mathbb{S}_{S}^{\mathcal{T}}
. However, this is not possible for any arbitrary choice of apparent horizon sphere
\mathbb{S}_{A}
. We prove the following result (see already Proposition
6.6
):

•

We can
choose

\mathbb{S}_{A}
so that it can be characteristically glued to a Schwarzschild trapped sphere
\mathbb{S}_{S}^{\mathcal{T}}
.

We note that it is difficult to glue two arbitrary spheres in a characteristic manner, but much easier if one has flexibility on one of the two spheres, which is what we exploit here. For comparison, Kehle–Unger
[
39
]
achieve gluing from a Minkowski regular sphere to the Schwarzschild event horizon, which already requires the use of the (difficult, albeit classical) Borsuk–Ulam Theorem. In the construction of
[
39
]
, the apparent horizon is null at the gluing sphere, which is consistent with the impossibility to spatially glue to a Schwarszschild apparent horizon in Section
1.4.1
. Our strategy, on the other hand, allows to prove the following (see Theorem
6.4
):

•

one can glue
\mathbb{S}_{R}
spatially to a Schwarzschild
trapped surface

\mathbb{S}_{S}^{\mathcal{T}}
as a
C^{k}
solution of the constraints.

This immediately proves Theorem
1.5
in the uncharged case (
q=0
) and serves as foundation for Theorem
1.2
.

1.4.3
Characteristic gluing of Schwarzschild to a Reissner–Nordström trapped surface

Then, we need to “charge” the spacetime, which we do via characteristic gluing (see already Theorem
6.9
):

•

one can glue
\mathbb{S}_{R}
characteristically to a Reissner–Nordström trapped sphere
\mathbb{S}_{RN}^{\mathcal{T}}
as a
C^{k}
solution.

In the above, one needs to assume that
\mathbb{S}_{RN}^{\mathcal{T}}
is a
sub-extremal
Reissner–Nordström
trapped
sphere. This is to be compared to the charged gluing result of Kehle–Unger
[
39
]
, achieving to glue a Schwarzschild regular sphere to a (possibly extremal) Reissner–Nordström event horizon of mass
M_{f}
, charge ratio
q\in(0,1]
under the condition:

\frac{|q_{0}|M_{f}}{q}\gg 1.

(1.13)

In Theorem
6.9
, we also require an analogous condition, but we remain away from extremality (
q=1
) as such

\frac{|q_{0}|M_{f}(1-q)}{q}\gg 1,

(1.14)

since we carry out
trapped spheres gluing
. This step of the proof is inspired from the methods and formalism of
[
39
]
, in particular the use of the Borsuk–Ulam Theorem on the sphere, that we already mentioned previously.
Combining this with the earlier results completes the proofs of Theorem
1.3
and Theorem
1.5
(case
q\neq 0
).

1.4.4
Gluing a Reissner–Nordström trapped surface to any dynamical event horizon

To prove Theorem
1.2
, we must go beyond spacetimes that are stationary in a neighborhood of
i^{+}
like those of Theorem
1.5
. To do this, we impose tangential event horizon data, which we trivially glue to the Reissner–Nordström trapped surface, providing it is only weakly trapped. The following summarizes Proposition
6.14
:

•

Let
\Phi_{H}(v)
an arbitrary decaying profile as
v\rightarrow+\infty
. One can glue
\mathbb{S}_{RN}^{\mathcal{T}}
to an event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
which is asymptotically Reissner–Nordström and such that the scalar field coincides with
\Phi_{H}
for large
v
, i.e.,

\phi_{|\mathcal{H}^{+}}(v)=\Phi_{H}(v).

We have now constructed the black hole spacetime in Theorem
1.2
to the future of its event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

1.4.5
Connecting the event horizon to an asymptotically flat end

To complete the proof of Theorem
1.2
, one must construct an asymptotically flat end. For this, we rely on spherical symmetry to solve (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) from left to right. The following result corresponds to Theorem
6.10
:

•

For any event horizon that relaxes to Reissner–Nordström sufficiently fast, and with sufficiently small charge, one can construct a corresponding complete null infinity
\mathcal{I}^{+}
and an asymptotically flat end
i^{0}
.

We note, however, that the left to right evolution is only relevant in a neighborhood of
i^{+}
, while the region near spacelike infinity
i^{0}
is treated by backwards-in-time evolution of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with prescribed scattering data at
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, see Section
6.4
. The proof relies on an application of the
r^{p}
method inspired by the author’s work
[
61
]
.

1.5
Scattering theory for charged scalar fields in the black hole interior

Construction/gluing results such as Theorem
1.2
described in Section
1.4
mark an important step towards the proof of Theorem
II
, in constructing a spherically symmetric black hole spacetime:

•

which is one-ended asymptotically flat (with no anti-trapped surface, and no trapped surface initially).

•

which relaxes to a sub-extremal Reissner–Nordström black hole.

•

which coincides with given
\mathcal{M}_{L}
near
\Gamma
. In particular, if
\mathcal{M}_{L}
is FLRW, it has a spacelike singularity
\mathcal{S}
.

However, it is not yet clear that the constructed spacetime even admits a non-empty null Cauchy horizon from infinity, which is crucial to apply Theorem
I
. The author previously studied black interior dynamics for (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) in
[
59
]
in the vicinity of
i^{+}
, interpreted as timelike infinity. To study this problem, one poses characteristic data of a future affine-complete outgoing cone
\mathcal{H}^{+}
interpreted as the black hole event horizon and a regular ingoing cone
\underline{C}_{in}
penetrating
\mathcal{H}^{+}
as depicted in Figure
10
and assume that
\mathcal{H}^{+}
asymptotically converges to a sub-extremal Reissner–Nordström black hole and that the following decay assumption holds for the scalar field

|\phi_{|\mathcal{H}^{+}}|(v),\ |D_{v}\phi_{|\mathcal{H}^{+}}|(v)\lesssim[1+|v|]^{-s},

(1.15)

in an Eddington–Finkelstein advanced-time coordinate
v
on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
as
v\rightarrow+\infty
. Then, the author proved in
[
59
]
the existence of a non-empty Cauchy horizon from infinity
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
as depicted in Figure
10
.

Theorem 1.9

(
[
59
]
, Theorem 3.2)
.

Consider spherically symmetric characteristic initial data for (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) on the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
converging to a sub-extremal Reissner–Nordström black hole and on a
C^{1}
-regular ingoing cone
\underline{C}_{in}
. Assume (
1.15
) holds as an upper bound on
\mathcal{H}^{+}=[v_{0},+\infty)
for some decay rate
s>\frac{1}{2}
.

Then, the spacetime is bound to the future by an ingoing null boundary
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}\neq\emptyset
(the Cauchy horizon) foliated by spheres of positive radius and emanating from
i^{+}
, and the Penrose diagram is given by the dark gray region in Figure
10
. Moreover, if
s>1
, then
\phi
is uniformly bounded and
g
is continuously-extendible.

Figure 10:
Local structure of the black hole terminal boundary near
i^{+}
for a spherical solution of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
).

Theorem
1.9
also shows scalar field upper bounds consistent with (
1.5
), but those are insufficient to obtain the more detailed estimates required by (
1.5
) as part of the assumptions of Theorem
I
. Therefore, to prove that (
1.5
) holds, we appeal to the nonlinear scattering theory developed by the author and Kehle in
[
40
]
. While the event horizon to Cauchy horizon scattering map is quite complicated for general frequencies, we identify a resonating frequency
\omega_{res}\in\mathbb{R}-\{0\}
(depending on the Reissner–Nordström parameters
(M,e)
) at which it takes a simpler form; informally,

\text{If }\phi_{|\mathcal{H}^{+}}\text{ oscillates at frequency }\omega=-\omega_{res},\text{ then }D_{v}\phi(u,v)\approx\phi_{|\mathcal{H}^{+}}(v)e^{iq_{0}\omega_{res}v}\text{ near }\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}.

In other words, if
\phi
oscillates at frequency
\omega=-\omega_{res}
at
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, then
D_{v}\phi
does
not
oscillate under
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
.

Nonlinear estimates were also employed in
[
40
]
to show that some aspects of the linear theory subsist in the nonlinear setting but, as written, the estimates in
[
40
]
are insufficient to verify whether (
1.5
) holds or fails for a given event horizon profile. Thus, building up on the methods of
[
40
]
, we establish a new nonlinear scattering result (Theorem
6.22
), whose rough version we give below.

Theorem 1.10
.

Let
\Phi_{H}(v)
satisfying (
1.15
) with
s>1
, and such that

|\partial_{v}^{2}\Phi_{H}|(v)\ll|\partial_{v}\Phi_{H}|(v)\ll|\Phi_{H}|(v)\text{ as }v\rightarrow+\infty.

Assume that

\phi_{|\mathcal{H}^{+}}(v)=\Phi_{H}(v)e^{-iq_{0}\omega_{res}v},

(1.16)

and that the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
is regular as in the assumptions Theorem
1.9
. Then, for every fixed
u
, and as
v\rightarrow+\infty
:

\begin{split}&|D_{v}\phi|(u,v)\approx_{u}|\Phi_{H}|(v),\\
&|D_{v}^{2}\phi|(u,v)\approx_{u}|\partial_{v}\Phi_{H}|(v).\end{split}

(1.17)

Therefore, to satisfy (
1.5
), it is clear that
one must choose

\phi_{|\mathcal{H}^{+}}(v)
to oscillate at the frequency
-\omega_{res}
(or equivalently choose
\Phi_{H}
in (
1.16
) that does not oscillate) so that
D_{v}\phi
does not oscillate towards the Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
, which is an essential requirement of (
1.5
); this is therefore an ad-hoc construction.

We conclude by emphasizing that it is conjectured that
D_{v}\phi
is oscillatory in the black hole interior for generic solutions of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
)(see, e.g.,
[
66
]
) which is why the solutions of Theorem
II
are non-generic
5
5

5

However, as explained in Section
1.1
, the solutions of Theorem
II
possess the main features of conjecturally generic solutions.
. Therefore, to require
D_{v}\phi
to be non-oscillatory in the black hole interior, as demanded by (
1.5
), it is crucial for our gluing strategy discussed in Section
1.4
to allow for the
prescription of (possibly non-generic) tangential event horizon data
, to ensure that the assumptions of Theorem
1.10
are satisfied. This strategy is at the heart of our approach.

Outline of the paper

In Section
2
, we introduce the necessary geometric preliminaries, together with relevant gauge choices and the Einstein–Maxwell–Klein–Gordon equations (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) in
(u,v)
-coordinates. In Section
3
, we provide precise statements of our main results. In Section
4
, we recall some essential results of
[
64
]
which we will be using in subsequent sections. In Section
5
, we show our conditional statements, i.e., we prove Theorem
IV
. In Section
6
, by far the most involved of the manuscript, is dedicated to the construction of one-ended black holes to which Theorem
I
applies, i.e., the proof Theorem
II
. Section
6
will also contain the proofs of Theorem
1.2
, Theorem
1.3
, Theorem
1.5
and Theorem
1.10
. Finally, in Section
7
, we carry out the construction of two-ended black holes to which Theorem
I
applies, i.e., we prove Theorem
III
.

2
Geometric preliminaries

The purpose of this section is to provide the precise setup, together with the definition of various geometric quantities, the coordinates and the equations that we will use throughout the paper.

2.1
Spherically symmetric solutions

We consider
(M,g,\phi,F)
, a regular solution of the system (
1.1
), (
1.2
), (
1.3
), (
1.4
), where
(M,g)
is a Lorentzian manifold of dimension
3+1
,
\phi
is a complex-valued function on
M
and
F
is a real-valued 2-form on
M
.
(M,g,\phi,F)
is related to a quadruplet of scalar functions
\{\Omega^{2}(u,v),r(u,v),\phi(u,v),Q(u,v)\}
, with
(u,v)\in\mathcal{Q}^{+}\subset\mathbb{R}^{1+1}
by

g=g_{\mathcal{Q}^{+}}+r^{2}\cdot(d\theta^{2}+\sin(\theta)^{2}d\varphi^{2})=-\Omega^{2}(u,v)dudv+r^{2}(u,v)\cdot(d\theta^{2}+\sin(\theta)^{2}d\varphi^{2}),

(2.1)

F(u,v)=\frac{Q(u,v)}{2r^{2}(u,v)}\Omega^{2}(u,v)du\wedge dv.

(2.2)

One can now formulate the Einstein equations (
1.1
), (
1.2
), (
1.3
), (
1.4
) as a system of non-linear PDEs on
\Omega^{2}
,
r
,
\phi
and
Q
expressed in the double null coordinate system
(u,v)\in\mathcal{Q}^{+}
:

\partial_{u}\partial_{v}\log(\Omega^{2})=-2\Re(D_{u}\phi\overline{D_{v}\phi})+\frac{\Omega^{2}}{2r^{2}}+\frac{2\partial_{u}r\partial_{v}r}{r^{2}}-\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{4}}Q^{2},

(2.3)

\partial_{u}\partial_{v}r=\frac{-\Omega^{2}}{4r}-\frac{\partial_{u}r\partial_{v}r}{r}+\frac{\Omega^{2}}{4r^{3}}Q^{2},

(2.4)

D_{u}D_{v}\phi=-\frac{\partial_{v}r\cdot D_{u}\phi}{r}-\frac{\partial_{u}r\cdot D_{v}\phi}{r}+\frac{iq_{0}Q\Omega^{2}}{4r^{2}}\phi,

(2.5)

\partial_{u}Q=-q_{0}r^{2}\Im(\phi\overline{D_{u}\phi}),

(2.6)

\partial_{v}Q=q_{0}r^{2}\Im(\phi\overline{D_{v}\phi}),

(2.7)

\partial_{u}(\frac{\partial_{u}r}{\Omega^{2}})=\frac{-r}{\Omega^{2}}|D_{u}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi|^{2},

(2.8)

\partial_{v}(\frac{\partial_{v}r}{\Omega^{2}})=\frac{-r}{\Omega^{2}}|D_{v}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi|^{2},

(2.9)

where the gauge derivative is defined by
D_{\mu}:=\partial_{\mu}+iq_{0}A_{\mu}
, and the electromagnetic potential
A_{\mu}=A_{u}du+A_{v}dv
satisfies

\partial_{u}A_{v}-\partial_{v}A_{u}=\frac{Q\Omega^{2}}{2r^{2}}.

(2.10)

Note that, under our electromagnetic gauge choice
A_{v}\equiv 0
(see (
2.37
)), (
2.10
) and (
2.5
) can also be written as

\partial_{v}A_{u}=-\frac{Q\Omega^{2}}{2r^{2}},

(2.11)

\partial_{u}\partial_{v}\phi=-\frac{\partial_{u}\phi\partial_{v}r}{r}-\frac{\partial_{u}r\partial_{v}\phi}{r}+\frac{q_{0}i\Omega^{2}}{4r^{2}}Q\phi-iq_{0}A_{u}\frac{\phi\partial_{v}r}{r}-iq_{0}A_{u}\partial_{v}\phi.

(2.12)

In terms of the radiation field
\psi=r\phi
, we have

D_{u}\partial_{v}\psi=\left(\frac{-\Omega^{2}}{4r^{2}}-\frac{\partial_{u}r\partial_{v}r}{r^{2}}+\frac{\Omega^{2}}{4r^{4}}Q^{2}+\frac{iq_{0}Q\Omega^{2}}{4r^{2}}\right)\psi.

(2.13)

Subsequently, we define the Lorentzian gradient of
r
, and introduce the mass ratio
\mu
by the formula

1-\mu:=g_{\mathcal{Q}^{+}}(\nabla r,\nabla r),

where we recall that
g_{\mathcal{Q}^{+}}
was the spherically symmetric part of
g
defined in (
2.1
). We can also define the Hawking mass:

\rho:=\frac{\mu\cdot r}{2}=\frac{r}{2}\cdot(1-g_{\mathcal{Q}^{+}}(\nabla r,\nabla r)).

Notice that the
(u,v)
coordinate system, we have
g_{\mathcal{Q}^{+}}(\nabla r,\nabla r)=\frac{-4\partial_{u}r\cdot\partial_{v}r}{\Omega^{2}}
. Now we introduce the modified mass
\varpi
which involves the charge
Q
:

\varpi:=\rho+\frac{Q^{2}}{2r}=\frac{\mu r}{2}+\frac{Q^{2}}{2r},

(2.14)

obeying

\partial_{u}\varpi=\frac{-2r^{2}\Omega^{2}}{\partial_{v}r}|D_{u}\phi|^{2}-q_{0}Qr\Im(\phi\overline{D_{u}\phi}),

(2.15)

\partial_{v}\varpi=\frac{r^{2}}{2\kappa}|D_{v}\phi|^{2}+q_{0}Qr\Im(\phi\overline{D_{v}\phi}).

(2.16)

An elementary computation relates the previously quantities :

1-\frac{2\rho}{r}=1-\frac{2\varpi}{r}+\frac{Q^{2}}{r^{2}}=\frac{-4\partial_{u}r\cdot\partial_{v}r}{\Omega^{2}}.

(2.17)

We also define

2K=\frac{2}{r^{2}}\left(\varpi-\frac{Q^{2}}{r}\right).

(2.18)

Now we can reformulate our former equations to put them in a form that is more convenient to use. For instance, the Klein-Gordon wave equation (
2.5
) can be expressed in different ways, using the commutation relation
[D_{u},D_{v}]=\frac{iq_{0}Q\Omega^{2}}{2r^{2}}

and under our electromagnetic gauge choice
A_{v}\equiv 0
(see (
2.37
)):

\partial_{u}\theta=-\frac{\partial_{v}r}{r}\cdot\xi+\frac{\Omega^{2}\cdot\phi}{4r}\cdot iq_{0}Q-iq_{0}A_{u}r\partial_{v}\phi-iq_{0}\partial_{v}r\cdot A_{u}\phi,

(2.19)

\partial_{v}\xi=-\frac{\partial_{u}r}{r}\cdot\theta+\frac{\Omega^{2}\cdot\phi}{4r}\cdot iq_{0}Q-iq_{0}A_{u}r\partial_{v}\phi-iq_{0}\partial_{v}r\cdot A_{u}\phi,

(2.20)

where we introduced the notations
\theta=r\partial_{v}\phi
and
\xi=r\partial_{u}\phi
.

Introducing the following notations

\begin{split}&\lambda=\partial_{v}r,\ \nu=\partial_{u}r,\\
&\iota=\frac{\Omega^{2}}{4\lambda},\ \kappa=-\frac{\Omega^{2}}{4\nu},\end{split}

(2.21)

we can also re-write (
2.3
) and (
2.4
),

\partial_{u}\partial_{v}\log(r\Omega^{2})=\frac{\Omega^{2}}{4r^{2}}\cdot\left(1-\frac{3Q^{2}}{r^{2}}-8r^{2}\Re(\frac{D_{u}\phi}{\Omega^{2}}\cdot D_{v}\bar{\phi})\right),

(2.22)

-\partial_{u}\partial_{v}(\frac{r^{2}}{2})=\partial_{u}(-r\lambda)=-\partial_{v}(r\nu)=\frac{\Omega^{2}}{4}\cdot(1-\frac{Q^{2}}{r^{2}}),

(2.23)

\partial_{u}\partial_{v}\log(\Omega^{2})=\kappa\partial_{u}(2K)-2\Re(D_{u}\phi D_{v}\bar{\phi})-\frac{2\kappa}{r^{2}}(\partial_{u}\varpi-\frac{\partial_{u}Q^{2}}{r})=\\
\iota\partial_{v}(2K)-2\Re(D_{u}\phi D_{v}{v}\bar{\phi})-\frac{2\iota}{r^{2}}(\partial_{v}\varpi-\frac{\partial_{v}Q^{2}}{r}).

(2.24)

Moreover, the following
\partial_{v}
-commuted equation will also be useful:

\begin{split}&D_{u}\partial_{v}^{2}\psi=\left(\frac{\partial_{v}\log(\Omega^{2})\Omega^{2}}{4r^{2}}[-1+\frac{Q^{2}}{r^{2}}+iq_{0}Q]+\frac{3\nu\lambda^{2}}{r^{3}}-\frac{\nu\partial_{v}\lambda}{r^{2}}+\frac{\Omega^{2}\lambda}{4r^{3}}[1-iq_{0}-\frac{5Q^{2}}{r^{2}}]+q_{0}r^{2}\Im(\phi\overline{D_{v}\phi})\frac{\Omega^{2}}{4r^{2}}[2Qr^{-2}+iq_{0}]\right)\psi\\
&+\left[-\frac{\Omega^{2}}{4r^{2}}-\frac{\partial_{u}r\partial_{v}r}{r^{2}}+\frac{\Omega^{2}}{4r^{4}}Q^{2}-\frac{iq_{0}Q\Omega^{2}}{4r^{2}}\right]\partial_{v}\psi.\end{split}

(2.25)

2.2
Double null coordinate and gauge choices

It is well-known that the above system of equations are invariant under the following gauge transformations:

\tilde{u}=f(u),

(2.26)

\tilde{v}=g(v),

(2.27)

where
f
and
g
are increasing
C^{1}
functions. Note that the gauge transform
(u,v)\rightarrow(\tilde{u},\tilde{v})
transforms the lapse
\Omega^{2}
in the following way

\tilde{\Omega}^{2}=\frac{\Omega^{2}}{f^{\prime}(u)g^{\prime}(v)}.

(2.28)

The system of equations (
1.1
), (
1.2
), (
1.3
), (
1.4
) is also invariant under the electromagnetic gauge transformation:

\phi\rightarrow e^{-iq_{0}F}\phi,

A\rightarrow A+dF.

where
F
is a smooth real-valued function.
|\phi|
and
|D_{\mu}\phi|
, on the other hand, are gauge invariant.

We will use several gauge choices throughout the paper, always for convenience of the proof; none of them will truly matter, as all the theorem statements are formulated independently of the gauge.

For the convenience of the reader, we regroup some of the gauge choices we will use in the sequel, and refer to this section when each choice if elected.

G.i

Let
\Sigma
be a spacelike hypersurface on which we prescribe
\partial_{v}r>0
and
\partial_{u}r<0
. This fixes the choice of

u_{|\Sigma_{0}}\text{ and }v_{|\Sigma_{0}},

(2.29)

and we then fix
(u,v)
in spacetime by requiring
u
and
v
to solve the standard eikonal equation

g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\Psi\partial_{\nu}\Psi=0

with the above initial conditions. This determines
(u,v)
in a neighborhood of the hypersurface
\Sigma
. This also provides a parametrization of the hypersurface
\Sigma
by

\rho=v-u.

(2.30)

G.ii

Let
C_{0}
be an outgoing, spherically symmetric cone. We can fix an unitary lapse on
C_{0}
, i.e.,

\Omega^{2}_{|C_{0}}=1,

(2.31)

which fixes, up to constant, the coordinate
v
, not only on
C_{0}
, but also in the strip
\mathcal{D}_{0}
obtained shooting ingoing (radial) null geodesics with starting spheres on
C_{0}
.

G.iii

Let
\mathcal{H}^{+}
be the event horizon of a black hole spacetime, which is affine complete towards the future. One can consider a future subset
\mathcal{H}^{+}_{v_{0}}\subset\mathcal{H}^{+}
, which is affine complete towards the future and with a past sphere
\mathcal{S}_{v_{0}}
. We then impose the gauge

\frac{-4\partial_{u}r}{\Omega^{2}}_{|\mathcal{H}^{+}_{v_{0}}}=1,

(2.32)

v_{|\mathcal{S}_{v_{0}}}=v_{0}.

(2.33)

As in the previous case, this determines
v
in the causal future of
\mathcal{S}_{v_{0}}
, and more broadly on a strip
\mathcal{D}_{v_{0}}
emanating from
\mathcal{H}^{+}_{v_{0}}
in the ingoing direction.

G.iv

In the same setting as before, let us assume additionally that
\mathcal{H}^{+}
is asymptotically Reissner–Nordström with mass
M
and charge
e
,
0\leq|e|\leq M
. Let
C_{v_{0}}
an ingoing cone emanating from
\mathcal{S}_{v_{0}}
towards the future or the past. We can fix the
U
-gauge

\partial_{U}r(U,v_{0})=-e^{2K_{+}(M,e)v_{0}}.

(2.34)

G.v

Let
\mathcal{M}
globally hyperbolic,
C_{u_{0}}
an outgoing cone and denote
\mathcal{B}_{u_{0}}=\mathcal{B}\cap J^{+}[C_{u_{0}}]
. We can fix the
u
-gauge in
J^{+}(C_{u_{0}})
by imposing

r\partial_{u}r_{|\mathcal{B}_{u_{0}}}=-1.

(2.35)

G.vi

Let
\mathcal{H}^{+}
be the event horizon of a black hole spacetime, which we normalize to be
\{U=0,\ v_{0}\leq v<+\infty\}
(this is not a
v
-gauge choice). We place ourselves in the black hole exterior region
\{U<0,\ v_{0}\leq v<+\infty\}
. Then, one can define the
U
-gauge as
U=-[2K_{+}]^{-1}e^{-2K_{+}u}
, and
u\in\mathbb{R}
is determined by

\lim_{v\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{4\partial_{v}r(u,v)}{\Omega^{2}(u,v)}=1.

(2.36)

As for fixing the electromagnetic gauge freedom, we impose throughout the paper

A_{v}\equiv 0.

(2.37)

(except, perhaps in notational passages such as Section
2.6
where convenience leads us to impose
A_{v}=A_{u}
. However, when using these notions later in the paper, we will work in an uncharged spacetime region where
A\equiv 0
anyway, so this difference in gauge will be inconsequential.)

We also have the freedom to fix

A_{u}(\cdot,v_{0})=0,

(2.38)

on some ingoing cone
\underline{C}_{v_{0}}
, which we will invoke when convenient.

2.3
Reissner–Nordström solution

Recall that the Reissner–Nordström solution is a spherically symmetric and stationary solution of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with
\phi\equiv 0
and
F=\frac{e}{r^{2}}dt\wedge dr
:

g_{RN}=-(1-\frac{2M}{r}+\frac{e^{2}}{r^{2}})dt^{2}+(1-\frac{2M}{r}+\frac{e^{2}}{r^{2}})^{-1}dr^{2}+r^{2}(d\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}(\theta)d\varphi^{2}),

(2.39)

with charge
e
, mass
M
and in the sub-extremal case
0<|e|<M
. Note that for (
2.39
),
\varpi\equiv M
and
Q\equiv e
.

r_{\pm}(M,e)
correspond to the area-radius of sections of the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and the Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
, defined by

\begin{split}&r_{\pm}(M,e):=M\pm\sqrt{M^{2}-e^{2}}>0,\\
&2K(\varpi=M,Q=e,r=r_{\pm}(M,e))=2K_{\pm}=\frac{2}{r_{\pm}^{2}(M,e)}[M-\frac{e^{2}}{r_{\pm}(M,e)}]\neq 0,\end{split}

(2.40)

and
2K_{\pm}
are respectively the surface gravities of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
. Note that, in the notations of Section
2.2
, and the Eddington–Finkelstein gauges (
iii
), (
iv
):

\begin{split}&\Omega^{2}=4(1-\frac{2M}{r}+\frac{e^{2}}{r^{2}}),\\
&\partial_{v}r=-\partial_{u}r=(1-\frac{2M}{r}+\frac{e^{2}}{r^{2}}).\end{split}

(2.41)

2.4
Trapped region and apparent horizon

We define the trapped region
\mathcal{T}
, the regular region
\mathcal{R}
and the apparent horizon
\mathcal{A}
as

1.

(u,v)\in\mathcal{T}
if and only if
\partial_{v}r(u,v)<0
if and only if
1-\frac{2\rho(u,v)}{r(u,v)}<0
,

2.

(u,v)\in\mathcal{R}
if and only if
\partial_{v}r(u,v)>0
if and only if
1-\frac{2\rho(u,v)}{r(u,v)}>0
,

3.

(u,v)\in\mathcal{A}
if and only if
\partial_{v}r(u,v)=0
if and only if
1-\frac{2\rho(u,v)}{r(u,v)}=0
.

Note that, if
\mathcal{M}
is the MGHD of initial data on
\Sigma
which has no anti-trapped sphere, i.e.,
\partial_{u}r<0
on
\Sigma
, then
\partial_{u}r<0
on
\mathcal{M}
, as a consequence of (
2.8
). We will only consider spacetimes free of anti-trapped sphere in this manner.

Note also that, if
\mathbb{S}_{1}\in\mathcal{T}
(respectively
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{T}
) and
\mathbb{S}_{2}
is located on an outgoing cone emanating from
\mathbb{S}_{1}
, then
\mathbb{S}_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
(respectively
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{T}
), as a consequence of (
2.9
). This well-known property will be used repetitively throughout the paper with no further reference.

2.5
Asymptotically flat one-ended initial data

We prescribe initial data
(r,f,h,l,Q,\phi,\dot{\phi})
on an hypersurface
\Sigma_{0}
diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and denote
\Gamma
the center (diffeomorphic image of the origin of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
), where
r\in C^{2}(\Sigma_{0})
,
f\in C^{1}(\Sigma_{0})
,
Q\in C^{1}(\Sigma_{0})
,
\phi\in C^{1}(\Sigma_{0})
,
\dot{\phi}\in C^{0}(\Sigma_{0})
. The induced metric
\hat{g}
, second fundamental form
\hat{k}
, Maxwell field
F
on
\Sigma_{0}
, are given by

\hat{g}=f^{2}(\rho)d\rho^{2}+r^{2}(\rho)d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{2}},

(2.42)

\hat{k}=h(\rho)d\rho^{2}+l(\rho)d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{2}},

(2.43)

(\phi,D_{n}\phi)_{|\Sigma_{0}}=(\phi,\dot{\phi}),

(2.44)

F(n,\partial_{\rho})_{|\Sigma_{0}}=\frac{Qf}{r^{2}},

(2.45)

where
n
is the unit future-directed normal to
\Sigma_{0}
. Moreover, we need to specify an electromagnetic potential
A
such that
dA=F
(electromagnetic gauge choice). For simplicity (note that this is different from the gauge choice (
2.37
), but this will not impact the subsequent results.), we can choose
A_{\rho}=0
, in which case (
2.10
) gives

\partial_{\rho}A_{n}=\frac{Qf}{r^{2}}.

(2.46)

Furthermore, (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) imposes the following constraint equations for
(r,f,h,l,Q,\phi,\dot{\phi})
on
\Sigma_{0}
:

R(\hat{g})-\hat{g}^{\alpha\mu}\hat{g}^{\beta\nu}\hat{k}_{\alpha\beta}\hat{k}_{\mu\nu}+(\hat{g}^{\alpha\beta}\hat{k}_{\alpha\beta})^{2}=2|\dot{\phi}|^{2}+\frac{2}{f^{2}}|D_{\rho}\phi|^{2}+\frac{2Q^{2}}{r^{4}},

(2.47)

\nabla_{\hat{g}}^{\alpha}\hat{k}_{\alpha\rho}-\partial_{\rho}(\hat{g}^{\alpha\beta}\hat{k}_{\alpha\beta})=2\Re(\dot{\phi}D_{\rho}\phi),

(2.48)

\partial_{\rho}Q=-q_{0}r^{2}\Im(\bar{\phi}\dot{\phi}).

(2.49)

The following definition of asymptotic flatness is inspired from
[
46
]
, Definition 3.1.

Definition 2.1
.

We say
(r,f,h,l,Q,\phi,\dot{\phi})\in(C^{2}(\Sigma_{0}),C^{1}(\Sigma_{0}),C^{0}(\Sigma_{0}),C^{1}(\Sigma_{0}),C^{1}(\Sigma_{0}),C^{1}(\Sigma_{0}),C^{0}(\Sigma_{0}))
is an asymptotically flat set of initial data for (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) if
(r,f,h,l,Q,\phi,\dot{\phi})
satisfy (
2.47
), (
2.48
), (
2.49
) and there exists
\epsilon>0
such that the following estimates hold as
\rho\rightarrow+\infty
:

\begin{split}&|r(\rho)-\rho|\lesssim\log(\rho),\ |\partial_{\rho}r(\rho)|\lesssim\rho^{-1},\ |\partial_{\rho}^{2}r(\rho)|\lesssim\rho^{-2},\\
&|f(\rho)-1|\lesssim\rho^{-1},\ |\partial_{\rho}f(\rho)|\lesssim\rho^{-2},\\
&|h(\rho)|\lesssim\rho^{-2},\\
&|l(\rho)|\lesssim 1,\ |\partial_{\rho}l(\rho)|\lesssim\rho^{-1},\\
&|Q|(\rho)\lesssim 1,\ |\partial_{\rho}Q(\rho)|\lesssim\rho^{-1-\epsilon},\\
&|\phi(\rho)|\lesssim\rho^{-1-\epsilon},\ |\partial_{\rho}\phi(\rho)|,\ |\dot{\phi}(\rho)|\lesssim\rho^{-2-\epsilon}.\end{split}

(2.50)

Remark 2.1
.

Note that
(r,f,h,l,Q,\phi,\dot{\phi})\in(C^{2}(\Sigma_{0}),C^{1}(\Sigma_{0}),C^{0}(\Sigma_{0}),C^{1}(\Sigma_{0}),C^{1}(\Sigma_{0}),C^{1}(\Sigma_{0}),C^{0}(\Sigma_{0}))
is sufficient to make sense of (
2.47
), (
2.48
), (
2.49
) in the sense of distributions. In fact, we will work with a
C^{k}
solution for any arbitrarily large
k\in\mathbb{N}
and thus (
2.47
), (
2.48
), (
2.49
) are satisfied classically as long as
k\geq 2
. If desired, it is possible to introduce a stronger notion of asymptotic flatness (involving the spatial decay of more derivatives) and prove that this stronger notion is also satisfied for the class of spacetimes we work with.

2.6
Sphere gluing in spherical symmetry

We recall some relevant concepts regarding the gluing of spheres for (
1.1
)–(
1.4
), which can be found in
[
39
]
. In the notations of Definition
2.2
, the reader should associate
(\rho,\omega,\varphi,q,a)
to
(r,\Omega,\phi,Q,A)
,
\rho_{u}^{j}
to
\partial_{u}^{j}r
, etc…

Definition 2.2

(
C^{k}
sphere data set, Definition 2.3 in
[
39
]
)
.

A
C^{k}
sphere data set for (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) is the following list of numbers

1.

(2k+3)
real numbers
(\rho,\rho_{u}^{1},...\rho_{u}^{k+1},\rho_{v}^{1},...\rho_{v}^{k+1})
with
\rho>0
,
\rho_{u}^{1}<0
.

2.

(2k+1)
real numbers
(\omega,\omega_{u}^{1},...\omega_{u}^{k},\omega_{v}^{1},...\omega_{v}^{k})
with
\omega>0
.

3.

(2k+1)
complex numbers
(\varphi,\varphi_{u}^{1},...,\varphi_{u}^{k},\varphi_{v}^{1},...\varphi_{v}^{k})
.

4.

(2k+1)
real numbers
(q,q_{u}^{1},...q_{u}^{k},q_{v}^{1},...q_{v}^{k})
.

5.

(2k+2)
real numbers
(a,a_{u}^{1},...a_{u}^{k},a_{v}^{1},...a_{v}^{k+1})
.

satisfying the following conditions:

•

\varrho_{u}^{i+2}
can be expressed in terms of
\varrho_{u}^{j+1}
,
\omega_{u}^{j+1}
,
\varphi_{u}^{j+1}
, and
a_{u}^{j}
for
0\leq j\leq i
by formally differentiating (
2.8
),

•

\varrho_{v}^{i+2}
can be expressed in terms of
\varrho_{v}^{j+1}
,
\omega_{v}^{j+1}
, and
\varphi_{v}^{j+1}
for
0\leq j\leq i
by formally differentiating (
2.9
),

•

q_{u}^{i+1}
can be expressed in terms of
\varrho_{u}^{j}
,
\varphi_{u}^{j}
, and
a_{u}^{j}
for
0\leq j\leq i
by formally differentiating (
2.6
).

•

q_{v}^{i+1}
can be expressed in terms of
\varrho_{u}^{j}
, and
\varphi_{u}^{j}
for
0\leq j\leq i
by formally differentiating (
2.7
), and

•

a_{v}^{i+1}
can be expressed in terms of
\varrho_{v}^{j}
,
\omega_{v}^{j}
, and
q_{v}^{j}
for
0\leq j\leq i
by formally differentiating (
2.11
).

We say the above
C^{k}
sphere data is
C^{k}
lapse-normalized if
(\omega,\omega_{u}^{1},...\omega_{u}^{k},\omega_{v}^{1},...\omega_{v}^{k})=(1,0,...0)
.

Remark 2.2
.

We note that, contrary to
[
39
]
, we make the assumption that
\rho_{u}^{1}<0
in Definition
2.2
, so that the relevant sphere is not anti-trapped

We note that every
C^{k}
sphere data set is gauge equivalent to a
C^{k}
lapse-normalized sphere data set
[
39
]
(recall Section
2.2
for a discussion of gauge choices).

Definition 2.3
.

A
C^{k}
sphere data set is uncharged if
(q,q_{u}^{1},...,q_{u}^{k},q_{v}^{1},...,q_{v}^{k})=0
and
(a,a_{u}^{1},...,a_{u}^{k},a_{v}^{1},...,a_{v}^{k+1})=0
. If
D
is an uncharged
C^{k}
sphere data set and
D^{\prime}
is a
C^{k}
sphere data set that is gauge-equivalent to
D
, then
D^{\prime}
is uncharged too.

We now turn to the definition of spacelike gluing; although we will only use it in the uncharged case, we formulate it the general charged case below. Note that (
2.56
) in Definition
2.4
below is formulated in the gauge
A_{\rho}=0
for convenience; however, it is easy to make a more general definition not imposing this gauge.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
, two
C^{k}
sphere data sets. We say that
D_{1}
can be spatially glued to
D_{2}
if there exists
D(s)
,
s_{1}\leq s\leq s_{2}
a collection of
C^{k}
sphere data sets such that
D(s_{1})
is gauge-equivalent to
D_{1}
,
D(s_{2})
is gauge-equivalent to
D_{2}
and, defining

\begin{split}&r(s)=\rho(s),\\
&\lambda(s)=\rho_{v}^{1}(s),\ \nu(s)=\rho_{u}^{1}(s),\\
&Q(s)=q(s),\\
&A_{u}(s)=a_{u}^{1}(s),\ A_{v}(s)=a_{v}^{1}(s),\\
&\Omega^{2}(s)=\omega^{2}(s),\\
&\varpi(s)=\frac{r(s)}{2}\left[1+\frac{Q^{2}(s)}{r^{2}(s)}+\frac{\lambda(s)\nu(s)}{\Omega^{2}(s)}\right],\\
&\phi(s)=\varphi(s),\\
&\theta(s)=r(s)[\varphi_{v}^{1}(s)+iq_{0}A_{v}(s)],\\
&\xi(s)=r(s)[\varphi_{u}^{1}(s)+iq_{0}A_{u}(s)],\end{split}

(2.51)

such that
r
is
C^{k+2}
,
\lambda
,
\nu
,
\Omega^{2}
,
\varpi
,
Q
,
\phi
are
C^{k+1}
,
A_{u}
and
A_{v}
are
C^{k}
, and they respectively satisfy

\partial_{\rho}r(s)=\lambda(s)-\nu(s),

(2.52)

\partial_{\rho}\varpi(s)=\frac{1}{2}[1-\frac{2\varpi(s)}{r(s)}+\frac{Q^{2}(s)}{r^{2}(s)}]\left(\frac{|\theta|^{2}(s)}{\lambda(s)}+\frac{|\xi|^{2}(s)}{|\nu|(s)}\right)+q_{0}Q(s)\Im(\phi(s)[\overline{\theta}(s)+\overline{\xi}(s)]),

(2.53)

\partial_{\rho}\phi(s)=r^{-1}(s)[\theta(s)-\xi(s)]-iq_{0}[A_{v}(s)-A_{u}(s)]\phi(s),

(2.54)

\partial_{\rho}Q(s)=-q_{0}r(s)\Im(\bar{\phi}(s)[\theta(s)+\xi(s)]),

(2.55)

\partial_{\rho}[A_{u}(s)+A_{v}(s)]=\frac{\Omega^{2}(s)Q^{2}(s)}{r^{2}(s)},

(2.56)

and for all
1\leq j\leq k+1
,
\partial_{\rho}^{j+1}r(s)
,
\partial_{\rho}^{j}\varpi(s)
,
\partial_{\rho}^{j}\phi(s)
,
\partial_{\rho}^{j}Q(s)
satisfy the respective equations obtained by formally differentiating (
2.52
), (
2.53
), (
2.54
), (
2.55
), (
2.56
), replacing
\partial_{\rho}
by
\partial_{v}-\partial_{u}
,
\partial_{v}\lambda
by
\rho_{v}^{2}
, etc…

If
D_{1}\in\mathcal{R}
,
D_{2}\in\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{A}
and
D(s)\in\mathcal{R}
for all
s_{1}\leq s<s_{2}
, we say
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
can be spatially glued within the regular region.

If
D(s)
is an uncharged
C^{k}
sphere data set for all
s_{1}\leq s\leq s_{2}
, we say
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
are spatially glued in an uncharged way.

3
Precise statements of the main theorem

3.1
The local result of our previous paper
[
64
]

We first recall the main result of
[
64
]
, which is formulated for local initial data on bifurcate hypersurfaces
\underline{C}_{in}\cup C_{out}=[u_{0},u_{F})\times\{v_{0}\}\cup\{u_{0}\}\times[v_{0},+\infty)
as depicted in Figure
2
. In the discussion below, the
u
-gauge is fixed by gauge (
v
), while the
v
-gauge is inherited from an Eddington–Finkelstein gauge (
iii
).

Theorem 3.1
.

[Theorem 3.1 in
[
64
]
].
Let
u_{0}<u_{F}
and
v_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
u_{\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}}=\sup\{u\in[u_{0},u_{F}],\ \underset{v\rightarrow+\infty}{\lim}r(u,v)>0\}
. We denote
\underset{v\rightarrow+\infty}{\lim}r(u,v)=r_{CH}(u)
for all
u\in[u_{0},u_{\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}}]
. Assume the following estimates hold on
C_{out}=\{u_{0}\}\times[v_{0},+\infty)
:

\begin{split}&r_{CH}(u_{0})>0,\ \lim_{v\rightarrow+\infty}\partial_{u}r(u_{0},v)<0,\\
