\displaystyle\frac{r_{+}e^{i\omega_{res}u}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\textup{p.v.}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Big[\mathcal{F}[(\phi_{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}\chi_{\leq v_{1}}e^{i\omega_{res}\cdot}](\omega)

\displaystyle\cdot\frac{\mathfrak{r}(\omega+\omega_{res})\partial_{v}^{2}\tilde{u}_{\mathcal{CH}_{\mathcal{R}}}(\omega+\omega_{res},r^{*})e^{i\omega u}+\mathfrak{t}(\omega+\omega_{res})\partial_{v}^{2}(\tilde{u}_{\mathcal{CH}_{\mathcal{L}}}(\omega+\omega_{res},r^{*})e^{-i\omega v})}{\omega}\Big]d\omega.

(6.317)

Recall from the proof of Lemma
6.26
that
\frac{du_{\mathcal{CH}_{\mathcal{L}}}}{dr^{*}}=O(\Omega^{2}_{RN}(u,v))
and
\frac{du_{\mathcal{CH}_{\mathcal{R}}}}{dr^{*}}=O(\Omega^{2}_{RN}(u,v))
. It is not difficult to also show that

\frac{d^{2}u_{\mathcal{CH}_{\mathcal{L}}}}{d(r^{*})^{2}}=\left([\omega-(\omega_{r}-\omega_{+})]^{2}-(\omega-\omega_{res})^{2}\right)u_{\mathcal{CH}_{\mathcal{L}}}+O(\Omega^{2}_{RN}(u,v))=O(\Omega^{2}_{RN}(u,v)),

(6.318)

\frac{d^{2}u_{\mathcal{CH}_{\mathcal{R}}}}{d(r^{*})^{2}}=\left([\omega-(\omega_{r}-\omega_{+})]^{2}-(\omega-\omega_{res})^{2}\right)u_{\mathcal{CH}_{\mathcal{R}}}+O(\Omega^{2}_{RN}(u,v))=O(\Omega^{2}_{RN}(u,v)).

(6.319)

Therefore, we end up with

\begin{split}&\partial_{v}^{2}\left(re^{i\frac{\omega_{res}v}{2}}\phi_{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}(u,v)\right)=-\frac{r_{+}e^{i\frac{\omega_{res}u}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{F}[(\phi_{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}e^{i\omega_{res}\cdot}](\omega)\omega\mathfrak{t}(\omega+\omega_{res})e^{-i\omega v}d\omega+\Phi_{error}^{\prime}(u,v)\\
&=-\frac{r_{+}e^{i\frac{\omega_{res}u}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{F}[\partial_{v}((\phi_{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}e^{i\omega_{res}\cdot})](\omega)\mathfrak{t}(\omega+\omega_{res})e^{-i\omega v}d\omega+\Phi_{error}^{\prime}(u,v),\end{split}

(6.320)

where

|\Phi_{error}^{\prime}|(u,v)\lesssim\Omega_{RN}(u,v).

Then, using again the notation
\Phi_{H}(v):=(\phi_{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}(v)e^{i\omega_{res}v}
and (
6.312
), we obtain

\begin{split}&\partial_{v}^{2}\left(re^{i\frac{\omega_{res}v}{2}}\phi_{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}(u,v)\right)=-\frac{r_{+}e^{i\frac{\omega_{res}u}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{F}[(\phi_{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}e^{i\omega_{res}\cdot}](\omega)\omega\mathfrak{t}(\omega+\omega_{res})e^{-i\omega v}d\omega+\Phi_{error}^{\prime}(u,v)\\
&=-\frac{r_{+}e^{i\frac{\omega_{res}u}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\left(\mathfrak{t}(\omega_{res})\partial_{v}\Phi_{H}(v)+\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}(\omega_{res})\partial_{v}^{2}\Phi_{H}(v)+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{F}[\partial_{v}^{3}\Phi_{H}](\omega)\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{\omega_{res}}(\omega)e^{-i\omega v}d\omega\right)+\Phi_{error}^{\prime}(u,v).\end{split}

(6.321)

Finally, similarly to (
6.316
), we obtain

\begin{split}&\bigl|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{F}[\partial_{v}^{3}\Phi_{H}](\omega)\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{\omega_{res}}(\omega)e^{-i\omega v}d\omega\bigl|\\
\lesssim&(1+|v|)^{-N}\left(\|(1+|v|)^{N}\partial_{v}^{3}\Phi_{H}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\mathcal{F}[\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{\omega_{res}}]\|_{L^{1}}+\|\partial_{v}^{3}\Phi_{H}\|_{L^{2}}\|(1+|v|)^{N}\mathcal{F}[\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{\omega_{res}}]\|_{L^{2}}\right)\\
\lesssim&(1+|v|)^{-N}\left(\|(1+|v|)^{N}\partial_{v}^{3}\Phi_{H}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\partial_{v}^{3}\Phi_{H}\|_{L^{2}}\right),\end{split}

(6.322)

which concludes the proof of (
6.308
), (
6.309
). (
6.310
) is obtained similarly.

∎

6.5.3
Recalling previous nonlinear estimates in the black hole interior from
[
40
]

We now retrieve estimates previously proven in
[
40
]
, Proposition 6.16. Strictly speaking, the estimates of
[
40
]
are stated assuming
s<1
, whereas here we assume to the contrary
s>1
, so rates of the form
O(v^{1-2s})
or
O(v^{1-3s})
are replaced by the weaker rates
v^{-s}
and
v^{-2s}
in the
s>1
case, respectively. We introduce the curve
\gamma
from
[
59
,
40
]
as
\gamma=\{(u,v),\ u+v=-\Delta^{\prime}+\frac{2s}{2|K_{-}|}\log(v),\ u\leq-\Delta\}
for some constant
\Delta^{\prime}>0
, and the notations
u_{\gamma}(v)
and
v_{\gamma}(u)
designed so that
(u_{\gamma}(v),v)\in\gamma
and
(u,v_{\gamma}(u))\in\gamma
.

Proposition 6.28

(
[
40
]
, Proposition 6.16.)
.

Let
u\leq-\Delta
. Then, for
v\geq v_{\gamma}(u)

|\partial_{v}\log(\Omega^{2})(u,v)-2K_{-}|\lesssim v^{-s},

(6.323)

|\partial_{v}r|(u,v)\lesssim v^{-2s},

(6.324)

|\partial_{v}\phi|(u,v)\lesssim v^{-s},

(6.325)

\bigl||D_{v}\psi|(u,v)-|D_{v}\psi_{\mathcal{L}}|(u,v)\bigr|\lesssim v^{-2s}.

(6.326)

\bigl||D_{u}\psi|(u,v)-|D_{u}\psi_{\mathcal{L}}|(u,v)\bigr|\lesssim|u|^{-2s}\log(u).

(6.327)

Moreover, there exists
\theta(u)
such that in the gauge choice (
2.37
)

\bigl|\partial_{v}\psi(u,v)-e^{i\theta(u)}\partial_{v}\psi_{\mathcal{L}}(u,v)\bigr|\lesssim v^{1-2s}.

(6.328)

Finally, denoting
\delta g=\{r-r_{RN},\ \log(\Omega^{2})-\log(\Omega^{2}_{RN})\}
,
\delta A_{u}=\{A_{u}-A_{u}^{RN}\}
and
\delta\phi=\phi-\phi_{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
, we have the following difference estimates (away from the Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
): for all
u\leq-\Delta
,
v\leq v_{\gamma}(u)
:

|\delta g|(u,v)+|\partial_{u}\delta g|(u,v)+|\partial_{v}\delta g|(u,v)+|\delta A_{u}|(u,v)\lesssim v^{-s},

(6.329)

|\delta\phi|(u,v)+|\partial_{u}\delta\phi|(u,v)+|\partial_{v}\delta\phi|(u,v)\lesssim v^{-2s}.

(6.330)

Then, we collect some extra estimates which follow from the analysis in
[
40
]
. These estimates are sub-optimal, but nonetheless sufficient for our purpose.

Proposition 6.29
.

Let
u\leq-\Delta
. Then, for
v\geq v_{\gamma}(u)
:

|\partial_{v}^{2}r|(u,v)\lesssim v^{-2s},

(6.331)

|\phi|(u,v)\lesssim|u|^{1-s}.

(6.332)

\bigl||D_{v}^{2}\psi|(u,v)-|D_{v}^{2}\psi_{\mathcal{L}}|(u,v)\bigr|\lesssim v^{-2s}.

(6.333)

Proof.

(
6.332
) is easily obtained integrating (
6.325
) in
v
. For (
6.331
), we invoke (
2.9
) which we write as

-\partial_{v}\lambda+\lambda\partial_{v}\log(\Omega^{2})=-r|D_{v}\phi|^{2},

(6.334)

and (
6.331
) then follows from (
6.323
), (
6.324
), (
6.325
).

Proving (
6.333
) is slightly more involved but follows from the same strategy as
[
40
]
, Section 6: we control the difference between
\partial_{v}^{2}\psi
and
\partial_{v}^{2}\psi_{\mathcal{L}}
with the help of (
2.25
). First, we prove (
6.333
) for
v\leq v_{\gamma}(u)
using the difference estimates (
6.329
) and (
6.330
) of Proposition
6.28
. Then, in the region
v\geq v_{\gamma}(u)
, one can integrate (
2.25
) using the rest of the estimates (including (
6.331
)) to estimate its RHS, and obtain

\bigl||D_{v}^{2}\Psi|(u,v)-|D_{v}^{2}\Psi|(u_{\gamma}(v),v)\bigr|\lesssim v^{-2s},

(6.335)

and, of course, a similar estimates holds for
\bigl||D_{v}^{2}\Psi_{\mathcal{L}}|(u,v)-|D_{v}^{2}\Psi_{\mathcal{L}}|(u_{\gamma}(v),v)\bigr|
. Combining the two estimates then gives (
6.333
) in the region
v\geq v_{\gamma}(u),\ u\leq-\Delta
.
∎

6.5.4
Completing the proof of Theorem
6.22
and Corollary
6.23

Theorem
6.22
follows immediately from Proposition
6.27
combined
with Proposition
6.28
and Proposition
6.29
, notably (
6.326
), (
6.328
) and (
6.333
) under the gauge (
2.37
). We then turn to the proof of Corollary
6.23
.

Proof.

The following estimate:

\begin{split}&v^{-s}\lesssim|D_{v}\phi|(u,v)\lesssim v^{-s},\\
&|D_{v}^{2}\psi|(u,v)\lesssim v^{-s-1}.\end{split}

(6.336)

follows immediately from Theorem
6.22
as a consequence of (
6.264
) and (
6.265
) combined with (
6.267
) given that
s>1
(note that we can choose
N=\lfloor s\rfloor+2
in (
6.264
) and
N=\lfloor s\rfloor+3
in (
6.265
)). Then, using the formula together with

\begin{split}&D_{v}\psi=rD_{v}\phi+[\partial_{v}r]\phi,\\
&D_{v}^{2}\psi=rD_{v}^{2}\phi+[\partial_{v}^{2}r]\phi+2[\partial_{v}r]D_{v}\phi,\end{split}

(6.337)

and (
6.324
), (
6.331
), (
6.325
), (
6.332
) concludes the proof of (
6.268
). To obtain (
6.270
), it is then enough to integrate (
2.9
), taking advantage of (
6.268
). (
6.269
) and (
6.271
) are obtained similarly.

Then, to prove (
6.272
), note that (
6.271
), valid for all
u\leq-\Delta
by Corollary
6.23
shows by (
2.8
) that for all
u\leq-\Delta

|\partial_{u}r|_{|\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}}(u)\gtrsim|u|^{-2s},

(6.338)

and thus,
|\partial_{u}r|_{|\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}}(u)>0
. This concludes the proof of Corollary
6.23
.
∎

6.6
Piecing everything together and application of Theorem
3.1

We are now finally ready to prove Theorem
3.5
as advertised at the beginning of this section.

Proof.

Let
\Phi_{H}
satisfying (
6.5
) for some
s>\frac{3}{2}
and
q\in(0,1)
sufficiently small. We apply Corollary
6.11
to construct an asymptotically flat black hole spacetime with initial data on
\Sigma
with no trapped or anti trapped spheres, with regular event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
strictly to the future of
\Sigma
, a Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
, and
\mathcal{S}=\{r=0\}
which is FLRW near
\Gamma
. Then, we impose that
\Phi_{H}
satisfies the assumptions of Corollary
6.23
and thus, by Corollary
6.23
, the assumptions (
3.1
), (
3.3
) and (
3.6
)–(
3.8
) of Theorem
3.1
are satisfied under the Cauchy horizon
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
for
u\leq-\Delta
. To show that
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
is weakly singular, we apply Theorem
3.4
, Statement
E
. Note that, because the spacetime is FLRW near
\Gamma
,
\mathcal{CH}_{\Gamma}=\emptyset
in the language of Theorem
3.2
(in other words, there are no locally naked singularities). Thus, we can apply Theorem
3.4
to obtain the desired black hole construction. This concludes the proof of Theorem
3.5
.
∎

Note that the second statement of Theorem
1.7
in the one-ended case immediately follows from Theorem
3.5
.

7
Unconditional constructions of two-ended spacetimes

In this section, we address the proof of the unconditional results in the two-ended case, i.e., Theorem
3.8
.

In the two-ended case, we are allowed to work with
q_{0}=0
, where the results of Luk–Oh
[
46
,
47
]
and Gautam
[
33
]
are available. In fact, the class of spacetime we construct is rather large (contrary to the one-ended case), since it applies to any generic solution which happens to have a non-empty crushing singularity
\mathcal{S}=\{r=0\}
. We note that this set of solutions we construct is open in some topology, since the property
\mathcal{S}\neq\emptyset
is stable to perturbations (by a soft Cauchy stability argument). We show additionally that the class of generic solutions such that
\mathcal{S}\neq\emptyset
is non-empty by a new argument relying of Theorem
1.8
.

In this subsection, we assume that
q_{0}=0
and that the scalar field
\phi
is real-valued and we consider a two-ended spacetime MGHD arising from admissible initial data
(\mathcal{M},g_{0},K_{0},\phi_{0},\phi_{1},e)\in\mathcal{G}
, where
\mathcal{G}
is the generic set (in smooth topology) on which
L^{2}
-averaged lower bounds hold on the event horizon, as constructed in
[
45
,
46
]
.

Before turning to the proof of Theorem
3.8
, we prove a lemma regarding the propagation of scalar field upper and lower bounds. Since these estimates follow from known techniques
[
22
,
45
,
48
,
59
]
, we will simply sketch the proof. We also note that the proof of the following lemma only applies to the uncharged scalar field case, i.e,
q_{0}=0
in (
1.1
)–(
1.4
). The charged scalar field case
q_{0}\neq 0
is more complicated and requires the use of Fourier transform, see Section
6.5
.

Lemma 7.1
.

We consider
C^{1}
initial data on
\mathcal{H}^{+}\cup\underline{C}_{in}
in the framework of Theorem
1.9
. Let
s>1
.

•

Suppose that as
v\rightarrow+\infty

|\partial_{v}\phi|_{|\mathcal{H}^{+}}(v)\lesssim v^{-s}.

(7.1)

Then, there exists
u_{s}<0
sufficiently negative so that for all
u\leq u_{s}
,
v
sufficiently large

|\partial_{v}\phi|_{|\mathcal{H}^{+}}(v)\lesssim v^{-s}.

(7.2)

•

Assume that (
7.1
) holds and moreover that as
v\rightarrow+\infty

\partial_{v}\phi_{|\mathcal{H}^{+}}(v)\gtrsim v^{-s}.

(7.3)

Then, for all
u\leq u_{s}
,
v
sufficiently large

\partial_{v}\phi_{|\mathcal{H}^{+}}(v)\gtrsim v^{-s}.

(7.4)

•

Assume that (
7.1
) holds and moreover that as
v\rightarrow+\infty

|\partial_{v}^{2}\phi|_{|\mathcal{H}^{+}}(v)\lesssim v^{-s-1}.

(7.5)

Then, for all
u\leq u_{s}
,
v
sufficiently large

|\partial_{v}^{2}\phi|_{|\mathcal{H}^{+}}(v)\lesssim v^{-s-1}.

(7.6)

Proof.

The proof of (
7.2
) is well-known, see e.g.,
[
45
,
59
]
; to give a brief sketch, we split the spacetime rectangle
[-\infty,u_{s}]\times[v_{0},+\infty)
into several regions where different estimates are proved:

•

the red-shift region
\mathcal{R}=\{u+v\leq-\Delta\}
for
\Delta>0
where red-shift estimates are exploited. Note that
\lambda<0
to the future of
\mathcal{R}
, and inside
\mathcal{R}
,
\lambda(u,v)\lesssim v^{-2s}
.

•

the no-shift region
\mathcal{N}=\{-\Delta\leq u+v\leq\Delta_{N}\}
for
\Delta_{N}>0
where a Grönwall argument is used.

•

the early blue-shift region
\mathcal{EB}=\{\Delta_{N}\leq u+v\leq-\Delta^{\prime}+\frac{2s}{2|K_{-}|(M,e)}\log(v)\}
for
\Delta^{\prime}>0
, where the logarithmic-size of the region is exploited.

•

the late blue-shift region
\mathcal{LB}=\{u+v\geq-\Delta^{\prime}+\frac{2s}{2|K_{-}|(M,e)}\log(v)\}
, where the blue-shift is exploited, specifically the estimate
\Omega^{2}\lesssim v^{-2s}
in this region. Note that
|\lambda|(u,v)\lesssim v^{-2s}
for
(u,v)\in\mathcal{LB}
.

For (
7.4
), we use a special monotonicity for (
2.5
) in the case
q_{0}=0
, first exploited by Dafermos
[
22
,
24
]
; we quickly sketch the main argument here. Integrating (
2.19
) in
u
, we get

\theta(u,v)=r\partial_{v}\phi_{|\mathcal{H}^{+}}(v)+\int^{u}_{-\infty}\frac{-\lambda}{r}\xi(u^{\prime},v)du^{\prime},

(7.7)

then integrating (
2.20
) in
v
gives

\theta(u,v)=r\partial_{v}\phi_{|\mathcal{H}^{+}}(v)+\int^{u}_{-\infty}\frac{-\lambda}{r}(u^{\prime},v)\xi(u^{\prime},v_{0})du^{\prime}+\int^{u}_{-\infty}\frac{-\lambda}{r}(u^{\prime},v)[\int^{v}_{v_{0}}\frac{-\nu}{r}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\theta(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})dv^{\prime}]du^{\prime}.

(7.8)

Note that
\int^{u}_{-\infty}\frac{-\lambda}{r}(u^{\prime},v)\xi(u^{\prime},v_{0})du^{\prime}=O(e^{-2K_{+}v})
if
(u,v)\in\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{EB}
(red-shift estimate on the initial data term
\xi(u^{\prime},v_{0})
) and
\int^{u}_{-\infty}\frac{-\lambda}{r}(u^{\prime},v)\xi(u^{\prime},v_{0})du^{\prime}=O(v^{-2s})
if
(u,v)\in\mathcal{LB}
. Moreover,
\frac{-\lambda}{r}(u^{\prime},v)>0
except if
(u^{\prime},v)\in\mathcal{R}
in which case
\lambda(u^{\prime},v)=O(v^{-2s})
. All in all, after bootstrapping that
\theta
remains positive, we get

\theta(u,v)\geq r\partial_{v}\phi_{|\mathcal{H}^{+}}(v)+O(v^{-2s}),

(7.9)

which then gives (
7.4
) as a consequence of (
7.3
).

(
7.6
) is slightly more subtle to prove. First, consider
\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
a solution of the linear wave equation on (
2.39
)

\Box_{g_{RN}}\phi_{\mathcal{L}}=0,

(7.10)

assuming the initial data on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
satisfy (
7.1
), (
7.5
). Since the Reissner–Nordström metric is stationary, one can commute
\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
with the Killing vector field
T=\partial_{v}-\partial_{u}
and then (
7.1
) is satisfied for
T\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
at the level
s+1
(since
[\partial_{u}\phi_{\mathcal{L}}]_{|\mathcal{H}^{+}}=0
, due to red-shift), so by (
7.2
), we have for all
(u,v)\in\mathcal{LB}
:

|\partial_{v}T\phi_{\mathcal{L}}|(u,v)\lesssim v^{-s-1},

(7.11)

and by (
2.5
) (applied on the Reissner–Nordström metric), we show that
\partial_{v}\partial_{v}\phi_{\mathcal{L}}
decays faster and thus we have

|\partial_{v}^{2}\phi_{\mathcal{L}}|(u,v)\lesssim v^{-s-1}.

(7.12)

Then, we argue as in Section
6.5
and compare the linear and nonlinear scalar field estimates: from Proposition
6.29
, we know that for
(u,v)\in\mathcal{LB}
,

|\partial_{v}^{2}\psi(u,v)-\partial_{v}^{2}\psi_{\mathcal{L}}(u,v)|\lesssim v^{-2s}.

(7.13)

and since
s>1
, (
7.6
) is proved as a consequence of
|\lambda|(u,v)\lesssim v^{-2s}
for
(u,v)\in\mathcal{LB}
and (
7.12
).
∎

Finally, we turn to the proof of Theorem
3.8
.

Proof.

We start with a two-ended spacetime MGHD arising from admissible initial data
(\mathcal{M},g_{0},K_{0},\phi_{0},\phi_{1},e)\in\mathcal{G}
. We will focus on the right-most side of the two-ended black hole, where we denote
\mathcal{H}_{R}
and
\mathcal{CH}_{R}
the event and Cauchy horizons, respectively; the left-side is treated analogously. First, (
3.7
) is trivially satisfied since
\phi
is real-valued. To show that (
3.1
), (
3.3
), (
3.6
) and (
3.8
) are satisfied for all
u_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}<u_{0}<u_{\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}}
, we first appeal to
[
33
]
, Theorem 1.15 (which itself relies on an application of Main Theorem 2 in
[
47
]
, upon showing its assumptions are satisfied) showing that on the event horizon
\mathcal{H}_{R}
:

\partial_{v}\phi_{|\mathcal{H}_{R}}(v)=Dv^{-4}+o(v^{-4}),\ \partial^{2}_{vv}\phi_{|\mathcal{H}_{R}}(v)=O(v^{-5})\text{ as }v\rightarrow+\infty,

(7.14)

for some
D\in\mathbb{R}
and
D\neq 0
from the fact that
(\mathcal{M},g_{0},K_{0},\phi_{0},\phi_{1},e)\in\mathcal{G}
, see
[
46
]
. As a consequence of (
7.14
), we can apply Theorem
1.9
to show that
\mathcal{CH}_{R}\neq\emptyset
, and symmetrically
\mathcal{CH}_{L}\neq\emptyset
. Finally, by
[
33
]
, Corollary 1.1.4 (which relies on
[
48
]
), we know that mass inflation occurs, i.e.,

\varpi_{|\mathcal{CH}_{L}}=+\infty,\ \varpi_{|\mathcal{CH}_{R}}=+\infty.

(7.15)

Then, by Lemma
7.1
, for all
u_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}<u<u_{s}
:

\frac{D}{2}v^{-4}\leq\partial_{v}\phi(u,v)\lesssim v^{-4},\ |\partial^{2}_{vv}\phi(u,v)|\lesssim v^{-5}\text{ as }v\rightarrow+\infty.

(7.16)

Therefore, (
3.1
) (note that the lower bound on
|\partial_{u}r|
in (
3.1
) is a consequence of (
7.15
), see
[
60
]
), (
3.3
), (
3.6
) and (
3.8
) hold for
s=4
as a consequence of (
7.16
).

Then, by Statement
A
of Theorem
3.7
, we have
\mathcal{S}_{i^{+}}=\emptyset
already. Moreover, define
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{G}
as the subset of spacetimes such that
\mathcal{S}\neq\emptyset
(we will show below that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
). Theorem
3.1
also shows that there exists
u_{T}<u_{\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}}
,
v_{T}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
(u_{T},u_{\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}}]\times[v_{T},+\infty)\subset\mathcal{T}
. Since
\mathcal{T}
is open, there exists
\epsilon>0
such that
(u_{T},u_{\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}}+\epsilon)\times\{v_{T}\}\subset\mathcal{T}
(note that since
\mathcal{S}\neq\emptyset
,
(u_{T},u_{\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}}+\epsilon)\times\{v_{T}\}\subset\mathcal{Q}^{+}
for
\epsilon>0
small enough). By the monotonicity of (
2.9
),
\mathcal{Q}_{+}\cap\{v\geq v_{T}\}\subset\mathcal{T}
. Therefore, there exists
u_{F}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\mathcal{Q}_{+}\cap\left([u_{T},u_{F})\times\{v_{T}\}\right)\subset\mathcal{T}
and
\lim_{u\rightarrow u_{F}}r(u,v_{T})=0
. Thus, we can apply Theorem
3.1
to the region
\mathcal{Q}_{+}\cap\{u\geq u_{T},v\geq v_{T}\}
, therefore there exists
\delta>0
such that
\mathcal{S}\cap\{u\leq u_{\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}}+\delta\}
is spacelike and the Kasner asymptotics (
3.9
)-(
3.13
) hold.

To show that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
, we will modify
\mathcal{M}
(of course, it could be the case that
\mathcal{S}\neq\emptyset
in
\mathcal{M}
already, but since we do not know that, we will modify
\mathcal{M}
regardless if whether
\mathcal{S}=\emptyset
or not) to obtain another admissible spacetime with
\mathcal{S}\neq\emptyset
. The strategy relies on the argument leading to the proof Theorem
1.8
, embodied by Proposition
4.1
. Let us fix
u_{\mathcal{H}_{R}}<u_{0}^{R}\leq u_{\mathcal{CH}_{R}}
. By Proposition
4.1
, we know that there exists a constants
C_{R}^{+}>0
,
C_{R}^{-}>0
,
v_{0}^{R}
(only depending on
u_{0}^{R}
) such that for all
u_{0}^{R}\leq u\leq u_{\mathcal{CH}_{R}}
,
v\geq v_{0}^{R}

C_{R}^{-}\ v^{1-2s}\leq|r^{2}(u,v)-r^{2}_{|\mathcal{CH}_{R}}(u)|\leq C_{R}^{+}\ v^{1-2s}.

(7.17)

In particular, for all
u_{0}^{R}\leq u\leq u_{\mathcal{CH}_{R}}
,
v\geq v_{0}^{R}

C_{R}^{-}\ v^{1-2s}\leq r^{2}(u,v).

(7.18)

Let
V_{R}\geq v_{0}^{R}
. There is, of course, the analogous notion on the left, with evident notations
v_{0}^{L}
,
u_{0}^{L}
,
U_{L}
.

We construct a new two-ended black hole manifold
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
as such:

1.

\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is the MGHD of admissible two-ended asymptotically flat initial data on a spacelike hypersurface
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

2.

\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\cap\{u\leq u_{0}^{R},v\geq V_{R}\}=\mathcal{M}\cap\{u\leq u_{0}^{R},v\geq V_{R}\}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\cap\{v\leq v_{0}^{L},u\geq U_{L}\}=\mathcal{M}\cap\{v\leq v_{0}^{L},u\geq U_{L}\}
. In particular,
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M^{\prime}}
have the same asymptotically flat ends and thus
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}
.

3.

As a consequence of the above,
\mathcal{CH}_{R}\neq\emptyset
and
\mathcal{CH}_{L}\neq\emptyset
in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{CH}_{R}
,
\mathcal{CH}_{L}
are weakly singular in the sense of mass inflation, i.e., (
7.15
) holds.

4.

\mathcal{S}\neq\emptyset
in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

Note that for (
3
), the Hawking mass blow up on
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
is clear in the regions
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\cap\{u\leq u_{0}^{R},v\geq V_{R}\}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\cap\{v\leq v_{0}^{L},u\geq U_{L}\}
, since they coincide with the corresponding regions in
\mathcal{M}
where mass inflation occurs on
\mathcal{CH}_{i^{+}}
by (
7.15
), and (
3
) then follows from the propagation of the Hawking mass blow-up towards the future. We now describe the construction and how to arrange for (
1
) and (
4
) to be satisfied. On the right side, let us pose initial data on the ingoing cone

\underline{C}_{V_{R}}=[u_{0}^{R},u_{F}^{R}]\times\{V=V_{R}\},

(7.19)

such that at the future end-point of
\underline{C}_{V_{R}}

0<r^{2}(u_{F}^{R},V_{R})<\frac{C_{R}^{-}}{4}V_{R}^{1-2s}.

(7.20)

Then, we consider the solution of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with
q_{0}=0
with bicharacteristic data on
\underline{C}_{V_{R}}\cup\left(\{u=u_{0}^{R}\}\times[V_{R},+\infty)\right)
and note that (
7.17
) is still satisfied since
C_{R}^{-}
only depends on the estimates (
3.1
) on the cone
\{u=u_{0}^{R}\}\times[V_{R},+\infty)
, which are unchanged. We claim that for the new solution in the spacetime region
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\cap\{u_{0}^{R}\leq u\leq u_{F}^{R},\ v\geq V_{R}\}
, the right Cauchy horizon breaks down, namely the following is true

u_{\mathcal{CH}_{R}}<u_{F}^{R}.

(7.21)

Indeed, if (
7.21
) did not hold, (
7.20
) would contradict (
7.18
). So (
7.21
) indeed holds, and by Proposition
5.1
, there exists
\mathcal{S}=\{r=0\}\neq\emptyset
, a future-spacetime boundary that can be attached to the spacetime region
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\cap\{u_{0}^{R}\leq u\leq u_{F}^{R},\ v\geq V_{R}\}
, which shows (
4
) is true.

To conclude, we still need to show (
2
) holds. For this, note that we have constructed two solutions of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
): on the right,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{R}
in the spacetime region
\{u\leq u_{F}^{R},\ v\geq V_{R}\}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L}
in the spacetime region
\{v\leq v_{F}^{L},\ u\geq U_{L}\}
. To connect these two, we can choose
(u_{F}^{R},V_{R})=(U_{L},v_{F}^{L})
and connect them at the sphere
(U_{L},V_{R})
. Then, we invoke local-well posedness for (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) with bicharacteristic initial data on

\left(\{U_{L}\}\times[V_{R}-\epsilon,V_{R}]\right)\cup\left([U_{L}-\epsilon,U_{L}]\times\{V_{R}\}\right),

(7.22)

and choosing
\epsilon>0
small enough, we obtain a solution of (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) in the spacetime rectangle
[U_{L}-\epsilon,U_{L}]\times[V_{R}-\epsilon,V_{R}]
. Moreover, noting that
(U_{L},V_{R})
is trapped, i.e.,
\partial_{u}r(U_{L},V_{R})<0
and
\partial_{v}r(U_{L},V_{R})<0
, by Cauchy stability, we have that

\partial_{u}r(u,v)<0,\ \partial_{v}r(u,v)<0\text{ for all }(u,v)\in[U_{L}-\epsilon,U_{L}]\times[V_{R}-\epsilon,V_{R}].

(7.23)

Thus, it is easy to construct a two-ended asymptotically spacelike hypersurface
\Sigma^{\prime}
which is admissible in the sense of
[
46
]
, i.e., that there exists
u_{L}^{ad}
,
v_{L}^{ad}
such that

\partial_{u}r>0\text{ on }\Sigma^{\prime}\cap\{u<u_{L}^{ad}\},\ \partial_{u}r<0\text{ on }\Sigma^{\prime}\cap\{u>u_{L}^{ad}\},

(7.24)

\partial_{v}r>0\text{ on }\Sigma^{\prime}\cap\{v<v_{R}^{ad}\},\ \partial_{v}r<0\text{ on }\Sigma^{\prime}\cap\{v>v_{R}^{ad}\}.

(7.25)

Moreover, we can arrange that
\Sigma^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
coincides with
\Sigma
in
\mathcal{M}
when the area-radius
r
is sufficiently large.

∎

Remark 7.1
.

Note that ensuring (
7.20
) requires a large scalar field on the ingoing cone
\underline{C}_{V_{R}}
(this is a consequence of the Raychaudhuri equation (
2.8
), see also
[
64
]
, Section 8 where this idea is discussed). Therefore, the construction of Theorem
3.8
is indeed a large-data one, which we know is necessary to obtain
\mathcal{S}\neq\emptyset
, since by Theorem
3.6
, small scalar-field data leads on the contrary to
\mathcal{S}=\emptyset
.
